Artificial Intelligence: Local Search





4.1 Introduction to Local Search

So far, what we have seen in the previous chapter is problem-solving. The procedure to solve any given problem is called problem-solving. In our exhibition hall example, how an intelligent robotic agent is trying to find the bomb is the key. How it identifies the shortest route between two different activation centers within the given exhibition hall? We ask the agent’s help to find the optimal and rational solution for the path-finding activity. Is this enough for an intelligent agent or an intelligent guy?

Just think about our real-time intelligence. No one gives the entire world’s details to us in real life. So, when we need to solve any problem, only some of the maps or the details about the world are available to us.

Imagine someone giving all the details about when we are born. It is easy to live here if all information is available before attempting solutions. With the help of this special database, our brain works ahead in uninformed and informed cases. Everyone works smartly on plan and execution when everything is available.

Nevertheless, it is not happening in the actual case. In the real case, everything is unknown. We do not have any heuristics or any data. Nothing is available. We have to search for elements and solutions within our neighbourhood only. The exploration will be there while traveling. Initially, we have to go individually, and we need to explore that. Therefore, the intelligence needs to explore the solution from the neighbourhood options.

Usually, the sequence will have surprising and unimagined outcomes in real life. Immediately, our brain considers the best solution with the calculated risks when we face problems. A similar situation comes to intelligent agents too. In many situations, the intelligent agent must make decisions only from the neighbourhood options.

Whatever the concept we have seen so far is called local search. The local search is always searching for solutions from the solution space itself. First, however, we must converge the solution in the solution world.

What is the difference between the general search we have discussed in the previous chapters and the local search we are discussing here? We can discuss this with the help of a simple example. Imagine our intelligent robotic agent is supposed to report the boss in five-star resorts in Yelagiri hills, Tamilnadu. Furthermore, all of a sudden, this call has been made. The robot needs to explore the roads and hills. It does not know anything. No one gives the map to the Intelligent agent. Even oral information also is not available. The agent is on the downside of the hill. Now it is seeing one direction board, which says that the right-side road leads to Bangalore by 130 kilo meters and the left-side road leads to Chennai by 250 kilo meters. Also, one more arrow mark says that the road is going towards Yelagiri by 15-kilo meters. No one has given any details so far, and the robot decides to go straight. Later after moving on the same road, the two roads get divided without any information. Many cars are going on one route where the other route is empty. Now robot must choose the best option to move. Here no one is available to help the robot. The robot needs to finish the task soon (As per the task, it needs to go top of the hill in 2 hours, and the robot’s owner is waiting for the robot at the top of the hill). A local search algorithm is considered the best option from neighbourhood options without knowing the subsequent route.

The robot must finish the travel at the top of the hill to reach the goal. That is the goal of robotic agents. The owner said he could not give any information about paths and routes. However, the goal is to reach that 5-star resort. So, it fixes the goal. After that, goal tests will be happening for every move.

Until the robot reaches the resort or the top of the hill, the analysis, goal test, and subsequent possible moves will continue in the robot’s travel. We are going to see this local behavioural search in this chapter.

See below Figure 4.1 for the solution space world.

Figure 4.1 Solution space world

The above Figure 4.1 depicts the local search. In our real life also, it is happening. Within this solution space only, the goals are there. However, we are entirely blind. We are still determining where we are now and what we are doing. We have only the neighbourhood options. In this solution space, we check the goal at every step. Once we find the solution in the solution space, our mission ends. However, sometimes, we see some solutions that may differ from the global maximum solutions we suppose to achieve. For example, suppose the owner has yet to give the resort details, and the goal is to reach the top of the hill; the robot may have six hilltop cases. It has solved if the robot reaches any of these six hilltops. However, this best solution is the global maximum solution, and other possible solutions are local maximum solutions.

Considering our real-life case, imagine we plan to reach out as a researcher in artificial intelligence. First, however, we need to learn how to get there. Currently, we can see only a few neighbourhood options, and these options are in front of us. For example, some business person is asking us to prepare the machine learning algorithm they need as a recommender system. Likewise, we have only a few options as neighbours. However, we are still determining whether it will take us to the solution we expect. We are considering the maximum option from our immediate neighbourhood. Finally, our travel ends in small-scale company owners or college professors. These are the leading local solutions we have received from the solution space. There must be more than these to reach the global maximum—most of the time. Our life will end in a local maximum.

Similarly, our robot also got struct. Many people on the reached hilltop said this is one of the tops on the hill. The robot gets confused and does not know whether the reached place is the global maximum. The robot did not reach the goal but completed it because the boss had just mentioned that it would be on the hilltop.

We never jump from the local maximum to the global maximum because jumping is not an option. If we must reach the global maximum, we must go down again. Refer the Figure 4.1 for details about this.

Most of the time, we also see the flat surface while moving in the neighbours’ steps. There will not be any improvement in our life, and our life is flat. We are frustrated by the flat improvement, which may be our bottleneck to reaching our goals. Example: We did not receive any improvement in our salary for the last five years in the job where we are working. Sometimes, we feel that our life also will go negatively, which is equal to going down the hill. Something like that, we lose money in life and start earning. This situation is climbing back case. We can see all these options in Figure 4.1

We will see some unique algorithms as part of local search. For example, we will see a hill-climbing search, simulated annealing, local beam search, and genetic algorithms. These are the powerful algorithms in local search. We will see all these one by one in this section.

Apart from connecting with the real-life example, the local search must be viewed from another angle. That is named objective functions with state space.

For example, in some problems and solution spaces, we need to improve our current state. We can measure this improvement from the objective function’s point of view. We can refer to one more example here to discuss more about this. Refer to the below Figure 4.2 for this.

Figure 4.2 Solution space solutions against objective function

We can consider one example here. There is one small town in India where the election will happen for the leader position in another ten days. We have only 16 vote-gathering employees available for one candidate to win the election. He has to gather the maximum votes from the available 250 streets. The limitation is just ten days. How to gather votes is challenging, and we need some brilliant ideas for this algorithm. He has hired our intelligent robotic agent for help.

Now the robotic agent is available in that town, planning to capture the maximum votes. The objective is to collect the maximum possible votes within ten days. We can correlate this with Figure 4.2. In that town, only 200,000 votes are there. Here the maximum of the objective function is to get 100,000 positive votes. Suppose all 16 votes gathering employees achieved collecting 100,000 votes; that is the global maximum. Here within ten days, if it is impossible to convince 100,000 votes and if it has reached 50,000 votes, this may be the local peak. That is the local maximum one. Nevertheless, there is a 100% probability of winning the election when we get 100,000 votes and only a 50% probability is there to win the election with 50,000 votes.

As per the algorithm, the intelligent robotic agent needs to design the best possible algorithm to achieve the goal with the help of 16 vote-gathering employees. How is it going to design the algorithm for this? Our robot is going to follow the local search algorithms. As per that, it asked all the 16 vote-gathering employees to go randomly to unique streets and start gathering the vote from any random house. However, after finishing vote gathering process, the employees ask the house members about the next possible house in the street where the employees get the maximum votes. After capturing this information, the individual employees will have more optimal options. These are neighbourhood options. Now the employee selects the maximum possible options by choosing many other factors like whether the house ideology belongs to the boss candidate, the exact thought process within all the family members, and whether it is easy to convince them.

So here, the objective function means vote count improvement. Once the vote-capturing process finishes, the employee will report it to the robot. The robot will add the vote count to the global count. So, it will increase the objective function. So constantly, the objective function will improve because the previous steps capture some votes.

There are options here like there will be a slowdown in the vote-capturing improvement from all the employees, and there will be flow movements where none of them reported the votes. (Flat surface in objective function). There may be degradation like people are rethinking the votes. Sometimes, if our local search algorithm is not an effective one, we may reach the maximum local hill and will get struct over there.

From an algorithm’s point of view in local search, artificial intelligence innovators have invented many algorithms like hill-climbing search, simulated annealing, local beach search, and genetic algorithms. We will see these algorithms one by one in this section.

4.2 Hill Climbing Local Search Algorithm

What is a hill Climbing local search?

As per Figure 4.2, if the algorithm makes the optimal decision from the neighbourhood options without any ahead knowledge, it is called local search. What is the outcome of these? The best amount the neighbours will get selected. From our example, the maximum number of possible votes is considered the optimal section of the house. This information the employee is getting from the current house. The current home representative may say that there are many characteristics in the street which are like the potential interests of the members for our candidate in the election. The employee selects the maximum values house from the maximum utility values for the next step. It will eventually increase the voting count. The total voting count increases.

Then the same activity gets repeated. The employee again captures the next set of possibilities from the next house and chooses the best one for the subsequent house selection. Optimal neighbour selection and execution move the state into hill climbing. The objective function will also grow toward the top of the hills with the blind objective. We aim to reach somewhere. In case we reach the maximum objective function, we are good. As mentioned earlier, the robot gathers data from all ten vote-gathering humans and accumulates the results.

This hill-climbing algorithm is also sometimes called greedy local search because this algorithm is getting executed without more thinking. It is just finding the optimal neighbour and just moving. So, it is a greedy one.

This hill climbing local search algorithm is getting struct in many situations. We can see these issues below.

Local maxima: The election is going to happen in 10 days. However, we have captured only 50,000 votes with the help of all 16 vote-gathering humans.

Local flat surface: Sometimes, after capturing the votes, no other votes are available in the next set of trials. With the help of all 16 employees, we could not achieve this. The possible reason might be anything else, like the targeted left-over people in the street belonging to some other parties.

Shoulder flat surface: In the middle of the improvement hill, we can struct with a local flat for a few moments. For example, only two neighbours are different from our ideology. The shoulder should not avoid the movements because we may go into global maximum after some time from this shoulder walk. Sideways is the solution for this issue. The algorithm will allow a few constant number steps before stopping the moves. The robot may tell employees to try for another ten steps with flat answers.

Reverse cases: From local maxima, we may go down due to negative campaigns. Alternatively, we purposefully make decisions to go down to restart again.

Local maximum to global maximum: Sometimes, we plan to go to global maximum from local maximum by coming down and going up. It may get looped.

There are other variants of algorithms also available for hill climbing.

One is stochastic hill climbing:

What is stochastic hill climbing means? Instead of selecting the best neighbour for the next move, we can get a random choice from the best neighbours. It means that when we have more similar or closely weighted top options, we choose anyone randomly instead of the best. It will help us to avoid selection time and analysis weightage. When we go in this mode, the convergence will not significantly improve, which will go in a zig-zag way. This zig-zag will increase the objective function and decrease it subsequently. This one touch all the maximum local hills in one shot. So, identifying the best route will get identified in the zig-zag movement. Revisit the logic one more time here. Instead of strictly selecting the best neighbour, we can select the best among the bests at random to avoid straight improvements. It may fluctuate but slowly converges to the highest hilltop.

The second one is first choice hill claiming:

The next one is the first-choice hill claiming. Imagine that one of the current house owners is saying that there are 50 good houses on that street, and all these houses provide good outcomes. Selecting the best one will not be possible because it is a costly operation in this movement. So, the employee can quickly select the first choice of neighbour instead of wasting time. We call this algorithm first-choice hill claiming. Here the convergence will go in a zig-zag way. Finally, however, it will reach the maximum one.

One last variety is random restart hill climbing:

Our core aim is to reach the global maximum or global solution. However, suddenly, if we find a local maximum, we want to be safe. So, we may restart the whole algorithm again by starting the game from another starting point. The starting point also matters. If we see in our example, the robot mentioned that the employee could choose any house as starting point when starting the algorithm. However, after reaching the local maximum, the employee might randomly restart the house from another street corner. It will help the thread reach the global maximum. We call this algorithm a random restart hill claiming.

4.3 Simulated Annealing

Whatever the zig-zag moments we have seen in the above algorithms belong to the regulated differential calculus approach only. The agent chooses only the optimal choice, and eventually, it ends up with a gradient decent approach. Instead of the optimal selection, if the agent performs the random selection with temperature probability, we call this a simulated annealing approach.

Note: What is a gradient decent approach? Converging into the optimal solution with the help of differential calculus is called the gradient descent approach. This approach belongs to the cost optimization part of mathematics.

So that we do not need to follow the gradient approach (Differential calculus regularity), and we can follow it like the ping pong game. Temperature probability introduces here to make the big zig-zag initially and slowly reduce the heat. It is straightforward, like a ping pong game. Initially, the temperature was high. So that more oscillation will be there. So slowly, the temperature will come down when the steps are moving. Based on the temperature, we decide the probability of selecting the randomness. From our example, we can discuss the above logic. Initially, the employee will choose the house randomly for the campaign. Then the heat is high, and the probability also is high. So, the next choice will be random. Slowly the heat will get reduced when the steps are moving. When the heat is getting reduced, the probability also will get reduced. Finally, the random choice will come down when the probability is less. Ultimately, the employee will follow the optimal choice instead of the random one.

What is the benefit of this algorithm?

When we oscillate our moves in all the areas of the solution space, we will reach the global maximum. So even though it delays, the probability of getting the solution (Global Maximum) is high.

4.4 Local Beam Search

Local Beam search is the next set of algorithms. So instead of only one state in the execution, we can keep K states. At a time, we can use K states in execution instead of only one state. That is the logic. In parallel, K states will move into the next successor and K information will get executed into the center. It means that it is not just the parallel random restart-based algorithm. We are not doing independent parallel threads in the execution. These parallel threads exchange information while in execution. It will make more benefits. Quickly the agents will change the path, based on the best route found. If we take our example, imagine we add more K helpers to our existing 16 people. These helpers will help in the same street. All helpers will choose random houses in the street. Once the first attempt finishes, all workers connect and discuss the route. They will find the best information and try attacking there. Suppose someone says that the north side of the street is better for campaigns based on the evidence, then everyone will choose that side for campaigns.

The next one is stochastic beam search. As with stochastic hill climbing, in beam search, if we choose random choices instead of the optimal ones, this is called stochastic beam search. We already know that beam search means ‘K’ helpers will do activities in parallel and exchange data at the end of the steps. So, everyone chooses the optimal path. However, even though it chooses the optimal path, it still maintains the optimal selection instead of a random one. It is the opposite in stochastic beam search. In stochastic beam search, there is a privilege for the ‘K’ workers to choose the random one from the best path. It means the discussions will be there after all the steps. Moreover, all ‘K’ workers will choose the optimal person’s route. However, they will not choose the best move in that path, and they will choose the random path.

4.5 Genetic Algorithms

Whatever we have seen above in stochastic beam search, the same logic will express one more additional way. This genetic algorithm is another variant of this. The stochastic beam search is the asexual reproduction algorithm. What does that mean? Instead of choosing a random move by one set of ‘K’ people, genetically, we can mix two sets of ‘K’ people’s movements and get the random child of logic. This child will have a decisive move. It is the subsequent move reproduction from the best move mix.

From our example point of view, imagine that all 16 employees, when they collect with their ‘K’ helpers, will exchange in-between data in the genetic algorithm and choose the best one in a reproduction way.

For example, first street helpers found that the north side has some clusters in the first street. Suddenly, the second street found a cluster on the north side of the second street and the south side. Here overall data will execute the robot. The robot will genetically reproduce the plan: it will ask first street helpers to mix their plan with the second street north cluster, too, instead of asking second street helpers to split. Likewise, our robot will ask many streets to mix their strategy. It will make good optimization in time and logic. As a result, the global maximum will get achieved.

Refer to Figure 4.3 below, explaining the logic behind this genetic algorithm. Also, we can discuss the steps of this algorithm in this example.

Figure 4.3 Example case for generic algorithms

steps in the genetic algorithm (As per the above example image)

  • The first step is the initial population and selecting the weightage of all threads. The robot will choose all parallel street workers’ weightage (Fitness).

  • Based on the weightage it chooses, it does the crossover. As mentioned above, the second street north cluster houses will get clubbed with the first street north side cluster. So that we save time and the global maximum will be achieved soon.

  • Mutation: Finally, some random alteration will execute with the help of small independent probability. (Random change)

Ultimately, the main objective is to achieve the global maximum solution.

05

Artificial Intelligence: Adversarial Search – Games





5.1 Game Theory

We also called the game theory an adversarial search. In real-life intelligence, we only solve some of our problems by ourselves. Most of the time, we get help from others, and cumulatively, we try to solve the problem. This logic refers to multiple-agent solutions, where all the agents connect and solve the problem. We also name this as the team playing. We also call these intelligence agents team agents. Sometimes, another agent may become an opponent in the multi-agent solution and the multi-agent intelligence. The intelligent agent may have human or other opponents negatively pulling the solution path.

Say we can recall our bomb threat example. In the bomb threat example, our intelligent robotic agent is trying to find where precisely the bomb resides in the exhibition. The terrorist may silently monitor the intelligent agent and divert the agent to reduce the agent’s winning probability in the solution activity. In action, both agents and terrorists are playing games. Both are opponents. This adversarial search means that the intelligent person or agent is trying to solve the problems, and the opponent tries to reduce the solution probability of the agent. How will these two activities happen? Just check with any game-related cases. The first person moves in this path towards a solution by calculating the success path and increasing the winning utility. The second person is the opponent on the same path and trying to pull the first person (Adding some obstacles to the first person) and decreasing the winning utility of the first person.

For example, recall our chess game procedure for reference. These are called adversarial game-based solutions. When we see the chess game as an example, what happens there? Before moving the coin, the first person in the game analyses all the subsequent possible permutations and combinations in mind. He will not only see the next step, and he also plans the subsequent steps. He may think about the opponent’s next set of moves and the impact on the opponent’s move from his moves. What is the best choice this first person needs to make will get decided based on so many permutations and combinations of logic in his mind. He also needs to think about the next move and will visualize the next ten moves in the game. Only masters are top in chess because they very well calculate everything about future predictions. Simply strategizing the game will take the first person to the winning state. So, intelligence is essential here. This intelligence is called adversarial search-based intelligence.

So, this intelligence needs to be addressed for artificial intelligence agents, too, and that is what this game theory is all about. In game theory, two parties are in solution space, and the intelligence agent tries to maximize his utility function and the winning possibility. Here utility function means the current state value. First, it adds the advantage values in the solution space. Then, it calculates the utility value (Example: Successful coin values of the agent in chess and failure coin values of the opponent, percentage of winning chance).

Then in the next step, the opposite person tries to minimize the winning possibility of the first person, and the first person calls this utility negative for him. It means that the second person will move the coin, and the move intends to reduce the utility value of the first person. So, this one automatically and indirectly increases the winning probability for the second person (Or opponent).

Here is the third step, the first person will come into play, and he will try boosting his utility. Finally, in the fourth step, the second person will come into play and try to reduce the first person’s utility. This one keep going across the game.

We usually denote these two people’s game theories as Max and Min game theory. The top utility booster is Max and maximizes the first person’s utility value. Similarly, the minimum utility reducer is Min and minimizes the first person’s utility value. Here the first person maximizes utility, and the second person minimizes the utility of the first person. This activity will keep happening on the game theory.

Figure 5.1 Exhibition Hall – game theory

Figure 5.2 Two-Player game tree

Figure 5.3 Three-Player game tree

The min-max activity will keep happening in the game theory. Refer to Figure 5.1. In the exhibition hall example, when the agent tries to move from the exhibition hall, the terrorist also tries to move from the restroom. The agent has two choices to move his coin in the solution space. Either he can move into the security gate or the Dance floor. The utility max of max player can be determined here. If the agent moves into the security floor, the probability of reaching the bomb calculates the utility value. Also, the completed portion gives utility value addition. Since the bomb search completes there, opponents’ values will add as an addition.

Now as we mentioned, the terrorist is watching the agent. He makes his move based on the decision that the agent has taken. Figure 5.1 shows all the possible actions. The move from the terrorist side reduces the utility value of the agent based on the probability and obstacles. This activity will keep happening until either one of them finds the bomb. If a terrorist finds it and he trigger the bomb. Agent losses the game. Suppose the agent finds the bomb and destroys the bomb. Agent wins the game, and terrorist losses. For every move, the utility functions recalculate in the tree. Here, this tree is the planning one and not the actual execution one. It means that the agent plans all these. If we go there, the terrorists may watch the agent, and terrorists might close the door. Once the game tree develops with the changing utility values, the agent finalizes the best optimal move by returning from the deadened leaves. The plan is like this. When the agent increases the utility value with this option, the agent’s opponent may choose that decision, reducing the agent’s power. In the end, who wins when we go in this solution route? There will be huge permutations and combinations.

Refer to Figure 5.2. It depicts the sample two-player game tree with the updated utility values. Figure 5.3 gives an idea about how three player game happens. Three-player or multi-player games also will happen based on the cases.

In the two-player game, when we say MAX is the agent, and MIN is the terrorist, we can call this as tow player MIN-MAX algorithm. The decision is called the minimax decision. Once the tree is ready, player one will search for the best option from backward analysis. If we need to find the bomb, the optimal previous step should be this. Even though an agent’s previous-to-previous step, the agent’s opponent takes any three options, the agent’s 4the previous step will give more power to the agent. Likewise, the agent needs to plan in reverse. The final optimal algorithm will be the final best one from the plan’s perspective.

What is the use of utility values? In the game theory, the MAX player chooses the maximum utility value among the available choices. MIN player chooses the minimum utility value among the available choices in the next step. The min player selects the minimum utility value because this utility value is the first person’s point of view. So, the MIN player’s objective is to reduce the utility value at the optimal core. So, he chooses the minimum value from the available options. (In reverse, this is the best choice to reduce the power of player 1. Similarly, the MAX player chooses the best maximum value to increase the power from the available choice.

In the actual execution, the terminal test will keep happening at every stage to identify if someone won. Then, finally, the game ends when terrorists have triggered the bomb. Then, positively, the agent ends the game by deactivating the bomb. So, in every stage, the agent will check whether the game completes at this stage.

The crucial understanding is this. In the game theory, the game tree will get planned before execution by adding the utility values. Once the tree is ready, the agent will start searching in the tree and find the optimal path by selecting the optimal MAX value and keeping the optimal MIN value from the opponents’ point of view. Once we keep searching in the tree with this mode, we get to know the outcomes. Based on the winning route, we guarantee that our winning will happen. Whatever the options the MIN player chooses, the probability will be on our side only if we have an excellent optimal plan.

Practically in only one case, we will lose in this game planning. If we had not planned well on the opponent’s side moves, this game planning would not work. Therefore, we intensely prepare the algorithm for our agent, and the agent will plan the game in the actual cases.

Utility value calculation goes with multiple options. If we hear a bomb sound in the particular activation center, our utility will increase. If the terrorist hears the bomb sound from his side, our utility will decrease. Likewise, many factors affect the utility values in our game planning.

5.2 The Minimax Algorithm

We start analysing and assigning the utility value in the reverse steps. If the MAX node is taking a decision, it just takes the maximum utility values from the terminal nodes. When the MIN node decides, it just takes the minimum utility values from the terminal nodes. Moreover, it goes back in analysis from terminal to root. Finally, the root node gets the optimal utility value from this algorithm.

The minimax algorithm follows a depth-first exploration of the game tree. ‘b’ legal moves at each point with ‘m’ depth provide the algorithm’s time complexity as O(bm) and space complexity as O(bm).

5.3 Alpha-Beta Pruning

Alpha-beta pruning is a basic pruning methodology in a game tree, which will help the agent to reduce the space and time required to analyse all the options in the tree. Furthermore, it means we can cut down the unwanted leaves and branches from the trees (We can prune the tree by tree pruning).

The idea is straightforward. The alpha-beta pruning will start the analogy from the bottom when we go up in the tree. We do not need the other option if we have one maximum possible utility value in the subtree. We can leave that low-cost option out of our analysis. So, we can prune it. For this, we are getting help from alpha and beta variables.

When we come from leaves in backward analysis, alpha is the highest value choice we have found at any checkpoint. In the same way, beta is the lowest value choice we have found so far at any checkpoint.

The rule is this. The alpha value should always be smaller than the beta in the game tree. Initially, we assign alpha as – Infinity and beta as + Infinity. When this rule violates the subtree or a node, we prune the subtree or the specific node.

The idea behind this is straightforward. Suppose we already got one maximum value (maximum utility option) in the MAX node. Then, this maximum will not work in the parent node because one minimum utility function is already available in the tree. In that case, the opponent never chooses this path. Therefore, we should not spend our time analysing this subtree. So, we are pruning this.

Similarly, we already got one minimum value (minimum utility option) in the MIN node, and this minimum will not work in the parent node because already one maximum utility function is available in the tree. In that case, we never choose our low-utility path. Therefore, we should not spend our time analysing this subtree. So, we are pruning this.

We will see this logic with one example tree. Please refer to the below tree (Figure 5.4). We give only the dead-end terminal values. We will assign the parent nodes from the minimax concept from terminal values. The alpha-beta pruning will have a little extra step. The steps are specified below:

Initially, at the first step, the alpha is – Infinity in the root node, and the beta value is +Infinity. Now the dead-end terminal has final utility values. We must move into the depth-first search concept to get the optimal route.

Figure 5.4 Alpha Beta pruning example

As per the depth-first search algorithm, we go to the next step. That is the MIN step, and we go to the next step because this is not the terminal one. However, while traveling, we keep assigning the alpha and beta values at nodes. So, in nodes, get beta as + Infinity and alpha as – Infinity.

Once we find the dead-end terminal node, the agent calculates the alpha value. Since this is a MAX node, the first value, 222, compares with alpha, and later alpha 222 will get compared with 223. So, the max value is 223. So, the alpha will be 223. Even though it has some value, alpha is always smaller than beta because beta is +infinity. So, the node value also is 223.

In the next step, the previous parent MIN will take the beta value as 223 because this is the MIN node. After that, beta values will get changed. Here alpha is still – Infinity. So, beta is still bigger than alpha.

Figure 5.5 Alpha Beta pruning example

When MIN goes to another child node, the beta value gets passed as 223. Initially, the Alpha is – Infinity. Then it gets updated as 225 because the first child has 225 for this max player. When alpha is 225, confusion starts for alpha and beta. The rule is this. Alpha should be smaller than beta. If it goes in reverse, the pruning will happen. The subsequent analysis is pruned at 225 > 223 (i.e., Alpha> Beta). We do not need to analyse the next value. Whatever the value is in the next child, it is not required because we already saw 225 as an alpha value more significant than our beta value. Why are we doing this? It is the MAX node. Only the maximum value will assign to alpha. If the next value is more significant than 225, already beta is smaller than 225. So, the new enormous value needs to make more sense to the rule. If small values appear next compared to 225, 225 is the dominant one in the MAX node. So, the alpha will not change. So, we are pruning all the leaves of this MAX node. We saved time and space by pruning with this logic’s help.

Figure 5.6 Alpha Beta pruning example

Then alpha and beta values go back into the root node. Since the root node is max, it updates the max value as 223 from the child node value. Beta is + Infinity since this is the MAX node. Alpha is smaller than beta. So, we check the next child of the root. The next child will go down to the dead end by updating alpha values as 223 and beta as – Infinity.

Figure 5.7 Alpha Beta pruning example

When we get the values back, the beta value in the MIN node gets updated as 221 since 221 is the max in the MAX child node (Compared to 220 and 221). Already the violation started in the MIN node. Alpha is bigger than beta. So, the entire subtree is getting pruned.

Figure 5.8 Alpha Beta pruning example

Now the root node value is 223. After pruning, we have saved lots of space and time in analysis. The final optimal root is 223 roots.

Figure 5.9 Alpha Beta pruning example

Figure 5.10 Alpha Beta pruning example

Artificial Intelligence: Logic and logical agents





Logic is a traditional concept in the artificial intelligence industry. Nowadays, we do not follow logic more extensively. The researchers moved ahead, and nowadays, they use probabilistic reasoning, which we will see in the next chapter. However, now we are going to see the traditional logic world.

When we say that logic is not in use, it does not entirely vanish. Still, people are using logic, and we will know why logic is optional at the start of the next chapter. We will also discuss how ahead we have moved in analysis. Artificial intelligence’s effective child machine learning has already gone very high worldwide. However, logic was helpful in the olden days. We are going to see all the options of logic in this chapter.

What is logic? Before we try to understand logic, we need to understand knowledge-based agents. These knowledge-based agents also are part of our intelligent agent types.

6.1 Knowledge-Based Agents

Whatever we have seen so far, it is related to searching. We were searching either in the problem world or in the solution world. Some cases that we have seen so far are detailed out below.

  • We are searching for solutions to reach our goal in the given program world.

  • The solutions are searching the solutions within the local solution world using local search.

  • We search for solutions when we have an opponent in the solution space.

However, we never discussed the stored knowledge within the intelligence system. Most of the time, we need an extensive library of knowledge within the brain system. Take an example of a human being. Our intelligence is also mainly related to the knowledge we store in the brain. It helps the intelligence system to infer the decisions.

The database of knowledge and how the database of knowledge is helping in the intelligent world are essential. If we revisit our human brain example, humans are always thinking a lot and driving the solutions when they get a problem in their path. Here we set the goal in our paths. We are trying to find the optimal algorithms which will move optimally. Here, intelligence also requires much information (Or the complete guide of domain knowledge) to achieve the goal optimally. Many inferences get derived from the stored knowledge base.

How has knowledge been stored in our brains? From where do we get all this knowledge? No one provides or inserts knowledge within our brains when we are born. Therefore, we must also find out what happened when we were born. We have yet to learn about the first year of our childhood. We cannot remember anything during that year because, at that age, our body parts were growing. When the brain slowly grows, it starts storing the data. The actual storage of information happens within the brain. Whatever the information was stored is referred to as knowledge. From this knowledge base, we derive lots of information.

Our intelligence system also should follow the same procedure for the knowledge base. Therefore, we must provide lots of knowledge-based details to our intelligence system. For example, if we see the agent, it perceives something. Then, it reacts or executes the actions using actuators.

So here, the set of rules has been given. So based on the rules, the simple reflex agent (you will see all these details in Chapter 8) gets the data through sensors (Input will get perceived through sensors) from the environment, and it executes the actions using the actuators.

However, we call these agents knowledge-based agents (That is what we need to say); the knowledge base will help to derive the solutions based on the stored knowledge. It will help the agent program through knowledge.

If we see our brain, we tell more data to the brain one by one (TELL) and ask for data to infer specific solutions (ASK). Whatever we have told our brain was stored. So whenever specific data for inference is required, the brain will refer to it from the brain database and pull some records for inference.

A similar way will get followed when we deal with knowledge-based agents. First, the agent program will TELL the knowledge into a knowledge base that was perceived through sensors (Perceived data). Then, the agent program will ASK the knowledge from the knowledge base, which is required for the agent program to make decisions.

So that is what is going to happen in the knowledge-based agents. When we say knowledge, it means it is a group of sentences. Sentences are something like the ones below.

  • “This is an apple.”

  • “This is an orange.”

  • “This is an apple or orange.”

  • “These are apples and oranges.”

  • “Brinjal and Orange are not in the same group. They are in different groups.”

So, the statements are getting defined in the knowledge base. So, the knowledge base is a complete set of sentences, and we need to specify the language as a medium. Even in our brains, we have internal language within our human system. The language inside our human body will be known to both the storage brain and brain execution system. When it gets stored, that language will be maintained. Then, when we ask for the data, the same language data will be received, and the execution part of the brain decomposes or understand the language.

So, Similar to this, the intelligent agent also must know representation language. The language will be the medium for all the components in the knowledge-based system. A knowledge representation language should store the data in the knowledge base in the form of the language syntax. So that is very important.

Whatever we have stored so far is referred to as background knowledge. Initially, the agent program informs the knowledge base (TELL) of whatever it perceives through sensors. The second step is this. It asks the knowledge base (ASK) what action we should perform. So much reasoning and analysis will go inside the knowledge base to provide the action list. Finally, the agent program TELL again to the knowledge base that the received action is getting performed now.

Sensors and actuators from one side interact with core representation and inference engine on the other end. Three crucial functions will get maintained internally for more communications. One is the percept sequence. What time the percept happens through sensors and the percept details will be informed to the knowledge-based inference engine when storing data. The second one is the action query. It is also the sentence. This sentence will ask knowledge base of what actions we need to perform at a specific time. The third one is the action sentence. It is also a sentence. This sentence will inform the knowledge base about the execution time of the actions performed based on the action set provided by the knowledge base.

Storing knowledge in the knowledge base also has two different approaches. One is the declarative approach. How is the data stored in our brains? Our brains will get the knowledge one by one through the TELL method. Our eyes and other sensors send the knowledge one by one in a declarative approach. Similarly, the intelligence agent follows a declarative approach, storing the data individually in the system.

The second method is called the procedural approach. As per this method, the entire data will get stored through the program. The entire batch program will store the data in the knowledge base. The declarative approach is better because it correlates the sentences. This approach will be good enough if already stored information acts as a new reference for the new sentences.

An exclusive example:

From now on, we need one example for reference. We can still refer to the same old intelligent robotic agent example, which is helping to find the bomb in the city exhibition hall. Our agent will find the optimal path to cover the entire hall and complete the actions immediately. It should happen before terrorist finds that. (Recall the old chapters, one terrorist is inside the exhibition hall, and he is trying to trigger the bomb). Here the agent program needs knowledge along with the knowledge based system, like how it stores the data.

We have 25 activation centers inside the exhibition hall. Therefore, we can store all the activity center details as knowledge in the knowledge base.

Performance measure: What is the performance measure for this knowledge base agent?

How soon the agent will find the bomb matters. What are all the decisions we need to take at these critical times? All these are performance measures.

Environment and actuators: There are 25 activity centers in the exhibition hall. The nearby area map should be there with the agent. Sensor: It is not only the eye camera sensor playing the sensor role here. It is also the hearing sensor. Based on the sound, the intelligent agent should make a decision.

6.2 Logic

Now we will discuss logic. Logic is the representation of knowledge using some languages. We have many languages, like Propositional logic and first-order logic. In this chapter, we are going to discuss propositional logic.

Before looking into propositional logic, we will initially see the basic information about logic. The logical representation must have syntax and semantics. Usually, any language must have syntax and semantics. Even our linguistic languages have syntax and semantics.

Syntax means grammar. It means the rules of the language. For example, we have components like subject, verb, and object in English. The grammar of English is related to the order in which we frame these components.

Moreover, semantics means the meaning of that sentence, like what we infer or what we understood from the correct sentence placed. So, if syntax (grammar) is perfect, we infer the semantics from the sentence. For example, in English, if we say “How are you?”, first will validate the syntax. As per syntax, the grammar is correct, and then we will analyse the sentence’s meaning. The meaning is, “Currently, an interactive person is asking about my overall happiness and health conditions.” Once the semantics are also proper, we will react based on the semantics. Finally, we will respond by saying, “I am fine.”

What will happen if the syntax fails? Say, for example, in English, if we say

“Engineer, and I am.”

– Here, the subject is coming in the third position, which is wrong. Therefore, we do not follow any syntax in the above sentence, and we cannot understand the sentence.

Once the syntax is acceptable, our brains will process the semantics. Semantics means the statements should be valid for all possible worlds in the logic world. The semantics defines the truth of each sentence concerning each possible world.

Let us consider the statement below.

X – 2 = 0

This statement is true in the world where X =2. The world X can be anything. It could be 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6. It has infinite worlds. Only in the “2” world the above statement will be accurate. The above statement is false for all other possible worlds (possible values).

The world should be either true or false. There are no other choices like intermediate value; we can use the name model instead of a possible world.

Imagine we have one sentence S1; The particular model M1 confirms this; then we say that M1 Satisfies S1. Alternatively, M1 is the model for S1. Per our previous example,

model 2 is the model for the “X – 2 = 0” statement.

Therefore, model 2 satisfies “X – 2 = 0”.

6.3 Logical Reasoning

There is a relationship between statements, which plays an essential role in inference and analysis. For example, if one sentence follows logically from another, we mention this as a logical entitlement.

S1 ENTITLES S2

It means sentence S1 entails sentence S2. Also, every model in which S1 is true will conform to S2. So, we can also say something like this.

S1 ENTITLES S2

IF AND ONLY IF

M(S1) WITHIN M(S2) (Statement model M(S1) will be there within statement model M(S2))

Example:

  • S1 → A = 1

  • S2 → A * B = B

  • M1 → The statement S1 is true for model A = 1. No other models will satisfy.

  • M2→ The statement S2 is true for models {A = 1, B = 0; A = 1, B = 1; A = 1, B = 2;}. However, none of the models will satisfy S2, where the model has different A values other than 1. For example, A=2, B=1 gets the result as 2, which is not satisfying.

    A=0, B=1 gets results as 0, which is also not satisfying

If we see the above statements

  • S1 ENTILES S2 because the model A=1 in S1 is the subset of models {A = 1, B = 0; A = 1, B = 1; A = 1, B = 2;}. So,

  • S1 ENTILES S2 BECAUSE M(S1) WITHIN M(S2)

  • Therefore, S1 ENTILES S2 IF AND ONLY IF M(S1) WITHIN M(S2)

6.4 Logical Inference

If we have two sentences, we are not only entitling the sentences. We also infer some meanings. It helps us to conclude our actions. The primary purpose of the knowledge base is this. We have lots of sentences in the knowledge base. We need to make some decisions from these sentences. It is called logical inference. We infer the data from two sentences.

Say, we have one minor knowledge base (KB).

KB (Knowledge Base in our brain):

  • S1 → The Man A has already died.

  • S2 → The man A’s son B got A’s job C.

  • S3 → B resigned from his father’s Job

  • S4 → Whoever resigns from job C they are joining the new job D.

Now after feeding the above KB in the brain, we are asking one question to our brain via a new sentence.

SNew → B in job D

Our brain is trying to infer this new sentence with the help of the Knowledge base by checking all the models.

Our new statement model will be like this

{A’s son B, C’s competitive Job D; A’s brother B, some excellent job D; A’s wife B, some remote job D}. There are so many models which will fit successfully for the new statement.

However, the Knowledge base also has only one model.

{A, A’s son B, A’s Job C, C’s competitive Job D}. All other models are false in KB. The false model will be like below

{A, A’s wife B, A’s job, C’s competitive Job D; A, A’s son B, some job C, C’s competitive Job D}

All models will be false in KB other than this.

Nevertheless, this model is in the new statements model list. It means that

“MODEL (KB) IS THE SUBSET OF MODEL (STATEMENT). THEN INFERENEC IS VALID.

It is called logical inference.

6.5 Inference Algorithm and Its Properties

For brain or Intelligent agent logic, whatever it might be, some algorithm is required to conduct inferences. Say for the above example, the inference algorithms will look like below.

  • “Go to S1 and understand that

  • Go to S2 and check whether B is the son of A

  • Go to S3 and check whether B resigned from the job

  • Go to S4 and check whether B is in new Job D”.

The inference mentioned above algorithm will help us to derive our new sentence as valid. Moreover, this is happening in KB.

So, a relationship exists between KB, ENTITLEMENT, NEW SENTENCE, AND INFERENCE ALGORIHTM.

We can write the relationships like below.

“KB ENTITLES NEW STATEMENT VIA INFERENCE ALGORITHM”

If KB is true, the new statement will be true. That is what entitlement says. However, who is helping this inference logic? “Our Inference algorithm helps new sentences to get inferred from KB via checking the entitlement.”

Now we will discuss the properties of this inference algorithm. Any inference algorithm in the world has two significant properties.

Soundness:

  • If the inference algorithm helps the new sentence to derive only the entitled sentences, it is called sound. It means It will get only true statements out.

  • So, a given logical system is sound if and only if the inference rules admit only valid formulas. Alternatively, if we start with valid premises, the inference rules do not allow an invalid conclusion.

  • We will discuss soundness via our example. If our inference algorithm can derive our new sentence (which is true), This algorithm is sound. If this algorithm can derive true sentences alone, this is sound. If any algorithm derives false sentences, that is not sound.

  • However, the inference algorithm is optional to cover all true inferences of KB.

Completeness:

  • If the inference algorithm can derive any possible sentences entitled in the KB, then this property is completeness.

  • Getting these kinds of super algorithms is complicated, which can derive all true entitlement.

  • Something like this. If we ask our brain whether B resigned from his father’s job, this complete inference algorithm will derive it quickly from KB. It can do anything and everything.

  • The super algorithm is either in the brain or the logical agent’s program.

  • Here adverse inferences are allowed. If the algorithm can get false derivation, that is ok, and that is not the problem. However, the excellent inference algorithm should confirm any false derivations in the Sound property.

  • Simple. In particular, if KB is true in the real world, then any sentence S1 derived from KB by a sound inference algorithm is also true in the real world.

6.6 Propositional Logic

Now in this chapter, we will also see one sample logic language which is getting used by our researchers. Propositional logic is simple and powerful.

Propositional logic (PL) is the simplest form of logic where propositions make all the statements. A proposition is a declarative statement that is either true or false. It is a technique of knowledge representation in logical and mathematical form.

Example Propositional sentences:

  • S1 → 8 + 8 = 16.

  • S2 → It is an apple.

  • S3 → 3 is the prime number.

Here are a few basic facts about propositional logic:

  • The Boolean logic will get used in Propositional logic. So, it works in 0 and 1.

  • Logic symbols like A, B, C, P, Q, and R will be used as symbolic variables to represent the logic.

  • Propositional logic can be true or false.

  • Propositions use logical connectivity via objects and relations.

  • Prepositions use logical operators like ¬ (NOT), ∧ (AND), ∨ (OR), →, ⇔

  • Propositions and connectives are the essential elements of propositional logic.

  • Connectives are logical operators that connect two sentences.

  • Questions, commands, or opinions are not propositions like” Are you going there?”, “Get the food,” “He may be right.”

6.7 Syntax of Propositional Logic

The syntax of propositional logic defines the allowable sentences for the knowledge representation. There are two types of Propositions:

  • Atomic propositions

  • Compound propositions

Atomic propositions are simple propositions. It consists of a single proposition symbol. These are the sentences which must be either true or false.

Examples:

  • 8 + 8 = 16 (True preposition)

  • “8 – 8 = 8” and “A – A = 2A” (False prepositions)

Compound proposition: Combining more straightforward or atomic propositions, using parenthesis and logical connective constructs, the Compound propositions

Examples:

If A is true, then C is true

If Raja is in a movie theatre, Raja’s friend Ravi is also there.

Logical Connectives:

It connects two simple propositions, and it represents a sentence logically. We can create compound propositions with the help of logical connectives. There are mainly five connectives, and the details are specified below:

  • Negation: ¬B is called negation B. The literal B can be positive literal or negative literal.

  • Conjunction: X ∧ Y, it is an AND operation. Two sentences can be in conjunction like Ram is a student and Ram is writing an exam. Both can connect via conjunction.

  • Disjunction: X ∨ Y. It is an OR operation. Two sentences can be in disjunction, like either Ram is a student or a lawyer. Both can connect via disjunction.

  • Implication: If one statement is true, the second one is also true, and it got implicated. A → B means A implies B. Raja’s statistics collection → Raja’s project report. Once the statistical collection is complete, the project report will be accurate.

  • Biconditional: A sentence such as A⇔B is a Biconditional sentence; for example, If the patient is breathing, he is alive.

    A = The patient is breathing, B = He is alive; We can represent this below A ⇔ B.

Table 6.2 Propositional logic elements

6.10 Inference Algorithm Logic, Theorems

General logic behaviour:

If the statement is true for all the models, it is called a tautology.

Say, for example, this statement always gets TRUE results for all the models

EO ∨ ¬EO

A proposition formula that is always false is called Contradiction.

EO ∧ ¬EO

Properties of Operators:

Commutativity:

A ∧ B= B ∧ A, or

A ∨ B = B ∨ A.

Associativity:

(A ∧ B) ∧ C= A ∧ (B ∧ C),

(A ∨ B) ∨ C = A ∨ (B ∨ C)

Identity element:

A ∧ True = A,

A ∨ True= True.

Distributive:

A∧ (B ∨ C) = (A ∧ B) ∨ (A-C).

A ∨ (B ∧ C) = (A ∨ B) ∧ (A-C).

DE Morgan’s Law:

¬ (A ∧ B) = (¬A) ∨ (¬B)

¬ (A ∨ B) = (¬ A) ∧ (¬B).

Double-negation elimination:

¬ (¬A) = A.

Rules of Inference in Logic:

What is Inference:

As mentioned, we can create the new logic from the old one. The old logic will be in our knowledge base. We can infer the new logic by referencing the old logic from our knowledge base. It means that we are generating conclusions from the evidence, and facts are termed inference. Prepositional logic helps us with this concept.

Inference rules:

We need to create a template for generating valid arguments. Inference rules are templates that will help us to infer from the knowledge base. In inference rules, the implication among all the connectives plays an important role. Following are some terminologies related to inference rules:

  • Implication: A→ B. → Boolean expression. If A, then infer B

  • Converse: The converse of implication, which means the right-hand side proposition goes to the left-hand side and vice-versa. It can be written as Q → P.

  • Contrapositive: ¬ A→ ¬ B → negation of A infers negation of B

  • Inverse: ¬ B → ¬ A

Types of Inference rules:

Modus Ponens:

If A and A → B is true, then we can infer that B will be true. We represent this as.

Modus Ponens →

Example:

Statement 1: “If he is hungry, he eats bread” & Statement 2: “He is hungry”

Conclusion: He eats bread

Modus Tollens:

If A→ B is true and ¬ B is true, then ¬ A will also be true. We represent this as.

Example:

Statement 1: “If he is hungry, he eats bread” & Statement2: “He did not eat bread”

Conclusion: This infers that he is not hungry.

Hypothetical Syllogism:

if A→C is true whenever A→B is true, and B→C is true.

Example:

Statement-1: If you have our car key, then you can unlock our car. & Statement-2: If you can unlock our car, you can bring it here.

Conclusion: If you have our car key, then you can bring it here.

Disjunctive Syllogism:

If A∨B is true, and ¬A is true, then B will be true. We represent this as

Example:

Statement-1: He either wins the game or losses the game & Statement-2: He did not lose the game

Conclusion: He won the game

Addition:

If A is true, then A∨B will be true

Statement1: I have blue pen & Statement2: I have a red pen

Statement 3: I have a blue pen or red pen

Simplification:

If A ∧ B is true, then B or A will also be true. We represent this as

Resolution: [It is vital to reduce symbols in inference]

if A∨B and ¬ A∧C are true, then BC will also be true. We represent this as

07

Artificial Intelligence: Uncertainty





7.1 What is Uncertainty, and How is It Useful?

Imagine our real life. For all our decision-making, we never guarantee the outcome without any obstacles because our life is uncertain. We plan and execute the actions. However, we cannot confirm that this will get executed properly without any obstacles. For example, we are driving the car and plan to reach the destination in 30 minutes. There will be infinite obstacles that may stop the car, like accidents on the road or car punctures.

We do not believe that information with 100% confidence, whatever the news or information we hear. For example, suppose Raja’s father calls Raja and informs him that he has transferred the money to his bank account. If Father calls and informs, everyone in the world will believe. However, no one is certain about their father’s message too. Uncertainty is something like this “There is a network issue, which did not allow the transfer,” “The father just franked him, “There is some problem in the destination bank.”

Say, we are tossing the coin. However, no one in the world can say that certainly (with 100% probability) the coin will get heads. We cannot say this because there are two possibilities in the coin. First, if someone knows how to throw the coin with some tricks, like that person who analysed the air moments of a particular coin’s shape, he could claim that there is a 100% probability. Even though we plan with 100,000 arrangements, we cannot deterministically say the event will have a 100% expected outcome. Still, it may show 98% certainty even if we have 100,000 arrangements before fixing any event.

Say, we are going to the bus station to catch the bus at 6 PM. We are confident that we will reach the bus stand and catch the bus without fail because we start from our home at 5 PM and the distance is just 10 Miles. However, even though everything favours our plan, we can’t 100% confirm the outcome. For example, all of a sudden, we might get one emergency call that we need to attend to, there is a big traffic jam in the path, which creates a delay in our plan, or the lockdown is going on in the bus stand due to some political party’s rally, sudden strike in the city.

Why are we discussing more examples here? We are discussing here that the logic will not work in reality. We have discussed logic in our previous chapter. As per the chapter, some languages insert all our knowledge into our brains. Whenever actions or decisions are required, we query the brain with the query statement. The query statement will infer the actions from the statements in the knowledge base. However, revisit this statement one more time. Do we think our brain is 100% confident about any inferred statements?

No. Practically our brain never confirms it. For example, our brain stored one piece of data long back: “If we put the tea powder in the milk, the colour of drinking tea will be brown.” Imagine we are visiting our relative’s house. When relatives serve tea to drink, we immediately ask our brains,

“I have one drink with me. The colour is brown. Is it tea?”.

Brian refers to the knowledge base and says,

“Cannot say. It could be tea or chocolate milk. For reference, consult nose. He will help you with the help of smell.”

Why is the brain not helping us make intelligent decisions because too many statements are deriving uncertain data? So, Logic, Logic statements, and Knowledge base will not work in reality and natural intelligence.

Similar issues are there with artificial intelligence also. For example, if the robot goes to a bus stand, it cannot guarantee that it will reach the bus stand by 6 PM even though it has enough belief, proof, and specific action plans.

Here what we need is probability. We need to make decisions with probability. In real life, also for all our pending cases, we take decisions or actions with the help of probability. For example, we need to decide whether this is a tea or chocolate drink. In that case, our brain makes decisions based on experience (Likelihood), available information (Knowledge base), and prior probability (60% of changes are there for tea). In real life, we make decisions based on probability and beliefs.

Here, we can ask one crucial question. Why can’t we feed all the details deterministically as a knowledge base? So that we can be 100% certain of these rules. Sometimes we need to deal with partial observability. If the person is blind, he believes that the drink is tea. He may get help from his nose for the smell. However, still, the participation of eyes is essential in belief. Even though everything is fully observable, the qualification problems mentioned above will happen. Like 100,000, data has to be handled in bus stand example to get a reasonable belief whether we reach the bus stand. (Like confirming that there is no accident, confirming that there is a rally planned on that day), The below idea is wise in all these situations.

The good idea is to realize the decision based on beliefs and entrain probabilities.

Why can’t we feed everything in the Logic system via the knowledge base? There are two main reasons for that to not choose as an option. These reasons are

  • Laziness: The size of the work is vast. One hundred thousand statements are required only for tea Logic. Memory usage also will be very high.

  • Theoretical and practical ignorance: Theoretically, we cannot feed all the statements as logic in some instances, like astronomy and police investigations. Even though theoretical ignorance is not there for domains like police investigations and medical, checking all the possible 100,000 tests with a suspected criminal is impossible. So, this is called practical ignorance.

So, a belief in the relevant statements will help in natural intelligence. So artificial intelligence also handles the same. The primary tool for the degree of belief is probability theory, which we will revisit in the following topic (we can also refer to this in the probability and statistics section. Chapters 10-14 in this book).

Probability helps us summarize the uncertainty from our laziness and ignorance.

To make some decisions, Initially, we need the choices. Then an agent must have preferences between the different possible outcomes of the various plans. We also use utility theory to represent the reason with preference. So, the agent will decide on probability and
utility.

Decision theory = probability theory + utility theory

Here utility means the degree of usefulness. Say, for example, we have five friends. We need to choose only one friend for our additional movie ticket. How to make the decision? All five are friends. So here degree of usefulness is matters. One among five is free now, and he considered watching this movie. Rest all have exams. So, the utility is high with this selected friend only. (For him only, the ticket is helpful).

Now probability theory also is required. As per the probability, there is an 80% chance that the selected person will not come to the movie because this theatre is too far from his house. On the other hand, the second person in the second order of our degree of usefulness has a 95% chance of coming for this movie. We combine both probability theory and utility theory to make decisions. Usually, our agents make rational decisions from the set of possible outcomes and actions with the help of utility and probability. We use the term maximum expected utility (MEU) in decision theory for making optimal decisions.
