We can also make normalized values for all the possible values of the random variables whenever required.

Like above, we can get answers to any query. In reality, the intelligent agent or our brain will have 200 * 200 possibilities-based join distributions. There will be 200 queries that ask our brain in reality. Our agent also asks 200 queries from joint probability tables.

7.12 Independence

We have already seen that if two events are independent (mutually exclusive), the conditional probability will be the independent probability, like below.

  • If A and B are independent, if only if

    • P (A/B) = P(A)

    • P (B/A) = P(B)

  • Here P (A ∧ B) = P(A) * P(B)

Like above, if we refer to our example, Body skin colour is entirely independent of the terrorist decision. Terrorists exercise well, and their body strength differs from the usual public. Terrorist may get tension when he sees the agent. So, body strength and body tension will become evidence for our analysis.

P (Terrorist, Body Strongness, Body Tension, Body Skin Colour) =

P (Terrorist | Body Strongness, Tension, Body Skin Colour) * P (Body Strongness, Tension, Body Skin Colour).

There are 48 possibilities from all the combinations since body skin colour is independent of everyone else at the table.

It means that

P (Terrorist, Body Strongness, Body Tension, Body Skin Colour) = P (Terrorist, Body Tension, Body Strongness) * P (Body skin colour)

We can eliminate this from this table. So, the final decomposed joint table will have only 12 possibilities. Therefore, we have saved 36 possibilities in our analysis and analysis time. Please see Figure 7.4 below for this decomposition.

Figure 7.4 Example – Probability Independence

The power of conditional independence is to reduce storage and analysis complexity. It applies to both our brains and intelligent agent.

7.13 Conditional Independence

P (Terrorist, Body Strongness, Body Tension) gives 23 – 1 = 7 independent entries.

If the person is a Terrorist, Body Strongness will be independent of Body Tension. Therefore, both of these pieces of evidence do not have any relationship.

In that case, if we want to get the probability for Body Strongness given the Terrorist probability and Body Tension probability, we can ignore the Body Tension probability. It is called conditional independence.

P (Body Strongness | Terrorist, Body Tension = True) = P (Body Strongness | Terrorist)

The same is applicable for

P (Body Strongness | Terrorist, Body Tension = False) = P (Body Strongness | Terrorist)

It means that the body tension of the terrorist never has any connection with body strength. Instead, body strongness has a connection with Terrorist probability. So, this conditional probability will help us to reduce two entries from 7.

Here, the probability of terrorists also will get a different perspective.

For example, now that we know Body Strongness and Body Tension are conditionally independent, we will have all four equations
below.

  • P (Body Strongness | Terrorist, Body Tension = True) = P (Body Strongness | Terrorist).

  • P (Body Strongness | Terrorist, Body Tension = False) = P (Body Strongness | Terrorist)

  • P (Body Tension | Terrorist, Body Strongness = False) = P (Body Tension | Terrorist)

  • P (Body Tension | Terrorist, Body Strongness = False) = P (Body Tension | Terrorist)

We can use directly the chain rule instead of full joint distribution [Recall Conditional Probabilities Multiplication Rule]. The simple way is this.

P (Body Tension, Terrorist, Body Strongness)

= P (Body Tension | Terrorist, Body Strongness) * P (Terrorist, Body Strongness)

= P (Body Tension | Terrorist, Body Strongness) * P (Terrorist | Body Strongness) * P (Body Strongness)

= P (Body Tension | Body Strongness) * P (Terrorist | Body Strongness) * P (Body Strongness)

This analysis still reduces our possibilities. All these are possible from conditional independence.

7.14 Bayes Theorem and Naïve Bayes

We have already seen this in the previous statistics section. Mr. Bayes innovated this theorem very effectively. We know that

P(A/B) = P(B/A) * P(A) / P(B)

What does that mean?

Posterior Probability = Likelihood * Prior probability / Evidence

From our intelligent agent’s point of view, if we need to get the probability of cause given effect, we need to get help from the probability of effort given cause.

P(Cause/Effort) = P(Effort/Cause) * P(Cause) / P(Effort)

Is it confusing? It is simple. Suppose we need to know the possible output (cause) for the actions we are trying (effort). In that case, we need to know the below likelihood history.

What and all the actions (efforts) have we taken when we got those possible outcomes in the past?

Here likelihood means to get all that old history and get that probability.

Here, the prior probability is causing probability. Without any evidence, what is the possibility of those outcomes in general?

The evidence is, of course, our effort’s probability. We have made that effort. Then, based on the effort probability, calculate the cause probability. So, evidence comes as a conditional one.

We will take one example here. Suppose an intelligent agent wants to know the probability of catching the bus when we use the car. In that case, it just uses the Bayes rule like the one below.

P (Catching Bus /Car) = P (Car/Catching Bus) * P (Catching Bus) / P (Car)

Here agent already knows to catch the bus. How did the car help in the past? One thousand times, it used a car in the past to catch the bus. Generally, the likelihood of using the car when the bus was the target is 0.50.

The prior probability of catching a bus in general (Either by using a car or any other vehicle) is 0.90. It is just the prior probability without telling what we use to catch the bus. In general, 90% of the time, we catch the bus.

Finally, what is our current evidence? We are using a car, and the probability of using a car is 100%.

So, P (Catching Bus /Car) = 0.90 * 0.5 /1 = 0.45 = 45%. So, we have only a 45% chance of catching the bus when we use the car.

If we ignore the evidence, the posterior decides from evidence and likelihood.

Posterior probability = α likelihood * Prior probability

Here α refers to the probability normalization constant (Making the sum to 1).

Now we need to analyse the case for two or more multiple pieces of evidence. If we take our example, getting the terrorist probability from both the pieces of evidence, we combine it like below

P (Terrorist | Body Strongness, Body Tenson) = α (0.02, 0.02) = (0.50, 0.50) → (After normalization)

It is good. However, not good when the evidence count grows. It is impossible to have a vast evidence count.

We need to use Bayes’ rule to reformulate the problem:

P (Terrorist | Body Strongness ∧ Body Tenson) = α P (Body Strongness ∧ Body Tenson | Terrorist) * P (Terrorist)

To solve the problem using the Bayes rule, we need to know the conditional probabilities of the connection.

“Body Strongness ∧ Body Tenson” for each value of terrorist.

Here we have only two pieces of evidence. So, it is possible to fetch the conditional probabilities. However, as we mentioned in the direct rule, this will become complex when we use many pieces of evidence. So, it is impossible to have colossal evidence.

Here conditional independence will help us.

P (Body Strongness ∧ Body Tenson | Terrorist) = P (Body Strongness | Terrorist) * P (Body Tenson | Terrorist)

We are concluding the above probability because Body Strongness and Body Tenson are independent and disconnected.

If we plug this into our Bayes rule formula, we get

P (Terrorist | Body Strongness ∧ Body Tenson) = α P (Body Strongness | Terrorist) * P (Body Tenson | Terrorist) * P (Terrorist)

The general definition of conditional independence of two variables, A and B, gives a third variable C, which is

P (A, B | C) = P(A|C) * P (B |C)

For our example, when we plug this conditional independence

P (Body Strongness, Body Tenson | Terrorist) = P (Body Strongness | Terrorist) * P (Body Tenson | Terrorist)

This formula is stronger than the previous conditional independence one.

Earlier in the previous section, we have seen that conditional independence provides equal statements like

P (A | B, C) = P (A | C) and P (B | A, C) = P (B | C)

From the previous section, the absolute independence assertions allow the full joint distribution decomposition into much smaller pieces. The same is true for conditional Independence assertions.

In our example, we can decompose the conditions like below.

P (Terrorist, Body Strongness, Body Tenson)

= P (Body Strongness, Body Tenson | Terrorist) P (Terrorist) → (As per Product Rule)

= P (c | Terrorist) * P (Body Tenson | Terrorist)

The above one is excellent; we can use this for the ‘N’ number of independent evidence.

When we come to our general model, this conditional independence helps us to derive like below.

P (Cause, Effect 1, Effect 2……. Effect n) = P(Cause) * P (Effect 1 | Cause) * P (Effect 2 | Cause) …………* (Effect n | Cause)

This is called naïve Bayes model. Also called as Bayesian classifier

Figure 7.5 Bayesian classifier – Naïve Bayes model

7.15 Bayesian Networks: Syntax

So far, we have seen the importance of independence and conditional independence. These will be our probabilistic representations of the world. However, our brain also reduces all these by conditional independence daily.

From now on, we will see the same concepts with the help of Bayesian networks. Bayesian networks help us get the systematic representation of the variable relationships.

In this section, we will discuss the syntax of these Bayesian
networks.

From now on, we can see one complex example of our probabilistic reasoning. We are still in the exhibition hall example. Now the agent is in the middle of the work. Suddenly, the agent gets one message from his engineer and the message saying, “As per CCTV, the terrorist has gone out of the exhibition hall.” Also, the intelligent agent gets one more message from the Police Investigation officer. This message also says the same content. Why has terrorist gone out? There are two reasons. 1. The bomb did not work when the terrorist attempted to trigger it. 2. The terrorist felt that he could not escape anymore.

In this example, we must make decisions or believe the message with probabilistic reasoning.

Why can’t we solve the above issue with logic?

As usual, the same reasons. The agent cannot make certain decisions from the received messages. Maybe some terrorist could have hacked the email system. If we get a message only from an engineer, it might be invalid. If we get a message only from the police investigation officer, we do not know him. Suddenly, one person is sending the message to the agent. How to believe that he is an officer?

Moreover, we need to analyse the possible reasons; we cannot make any decisions with 100% certainty. For example, we get only one message from the officer. What is the probability that we will get a call from our engineer?

If the officer had seen this on CCTV, would he have had some time to message the agent? Instead, he would be busy catching the
terrorist.

If the engineer and officer sent the message, is it more likely to guess the reason? The reason is essential here. If terrorists tried the bomb and it did not work, we can relax in searching for the bomb. Can we confirm this reason with the help of both messages? No. We cannot do this with the help of logic.

If the bomb is not triggered, we must continue the work because it may auto-trigger. However, we have yet to learn about that.

We also kept the cameras in the explored activation centers. That is why terrorists would have felt that they could not escape anymore. However, unfortunately, we have yet to find out about this one.

As we mentioned, we need to keep all these 100,000 possibilities in logic to make deterministic decisions. As mentioned in the previous sections, we must use probabilistic reasoning regarding laziness and ignorance (Theoretical and practical ignorance). There are many independences and conditional independence available in this example too.

So, we are going to use Bayes Net to make it easy.

What are Bayes nets?

In general, joint distribution P over a set of variables (X1, X2, XN) requires exponential space for representation & inference.

  • Bayesian nets provide a graphical representation of conditional independence relations in P.

  • Usually quite compact.

  • Requires assessment of only fewer parameters – Those are pretty natural.

  • The inference is efficient in belief networks.

  • Here, the topology will help us a lot.

Figure 7.6 Example – Probability Independence

From our previous section, we get the topology like the above Figure 7.6. “Body skin colour” is entirely independent of all other variables. Likewise, “Body strongness” and “Body Tenson” are conditionally independent of each other given “Terrorist probability.”

We need more connected Bayes’ net topology. The syntax of that is this.

  • A set of nodes with random variables are specified below.

  • A directed acyclic graph.

  • The conditional distribution for each node given its parents: P (Xi | Parents (Xi)).

  • For discrete variables, conditional probability table (CPT) = distribution over Xi for each combination of parent values.

Below is the Bayesian network (Figure 7.7) for our example, which we discuss in this section.

Figure 7.7 Example – Bayesian networks

7.16 Bayesian Networks: Factorization

Figure 7.8 Example – Bayesian networks with conditional probabilities

We need to assign probabilities for all the random variables. We need to recall our discrete random example concepts here. We also mentioned that the probabilistic reasoning would happen with all the possible random variables and full joint conditional tables. Referring to Figure 7.8, we have mentioned random variables and conditional probability.

Initially, we mentioned the reasons. The option “The bomb did not work when the terrorist attempted to trigger” has been mentioned with a random variable “A”. Random variable A has two possibilities. Either this statement is true or false. If true, the value is 0.45; if false, it will be 0.55.

Similarly, we have mentioned the probabilities for random variable F (“The terrorist felt that he could not escape anymore”).

The next is the joint probability, and the variable is T (“The terrorist has gone out”). It is the conditional probability and all four previous parents’ options (2 2 possibilities). These parents are pieces of evidence in conditional probability. For all the options (TT, TF, FT, FF), we have conditional probability (P (T | A, F).

Finally, we calculate the message’s probability with the random variables P and E. The parent is Terrorist Escape (T). So, these random variables have two possibilities.

Benefits:

Recall the full joint table option. If we use that old option, all five variables need 32 parameters in the table. However, using this Bayesian network, we will use only ten parameters (see Figure 7.8). How did we achieve this? Because of conditional independence.

Here in this example, if we know the terrorist has escaped, the probability of the messages depends only on the escape, and these messages are not depending the reason why he escaped. We have reduced from traditional joint probability distribution tables.

Suppose we need the probability for the engineer’s message; it will be

P (E | A, E, T, P) = P (E | T)

7.17 Inference in Bayesian Networks

Efficient inferences are possible with these Bayesian networks. Again, the graphical independence representation helps us.

We generally want to compute the marginal probability with the required evidence.

7.18 Bayesian Network: Conditional Independence

Conditional independence:

Figure 7.9 Example – Bayesian networks with conditional independence

Descendants are the connections in the topological networks. The topological semantics also say that all the variables are conditionally independent for all his non-descendants. What are non-descendants here? The super parents can be considered non-descendants.

See the Figure 7.9. Here Police Investigation officer’s message is entirely independent of all other than his direct father node, “Terrorist Escape.”

It is called conditional independence.

7.19 Markov Blanket

Markov blanket deals the independence differently.

Each node is conditionally independent of all others, given its Markov blanket:

  • parents

  • children

  • children’s parents

  • MB(A) = PARENT (A) + CHILDS (A) + PARENT (CHILDS(X))

Here children and children’s other parents are also gets included.

Imagine that in our example, the random variable A (“The bomb did not work”) has some parents. Then the parents of this variable A and the child “Terrorist escaped, “and the child’s other parent “Terrorist’s fear” all come to the dependent area of this variable.

7.20 Definition (D-Separation)

Imagine two variables, A and B, and a set of observed variables, C.

A set of variables C “d-separates” variables A and B if C blocks every un-directed path between A and B in the network.

To determine whether A and B are independent given the observed variables C, we can verify whether C d-separates A and B. If d-separation holds, then the independence relationship holds as well.

Points to remember in d-separation

First, we must consider every un-directed path between A and B to verify the definition. The word “un-directed” means that we do not care about the direction of the arrows on the path. Instead, we will consider it a path as long as a series of nodes and edges connect
A and B.

Second, there may be multiple paths between A and B. Therefore, we must consider every path and verify that C blocks every path between A and B.

Third, each path could have multiple nodes between A and B. The path is blocked if at least one node blocks the path. We can move on to a different path as soon as we find one node blocking the path. In the worst case, we need to check every node and discover that none of the nodes blocks the path.

Given this definition, our task boils down to the following: pick a path between A and B and, pick a node on the path, determine whether the node blocks the path.

Figure 7.10 Example – D Separation algorithm

Case 1: If C, we block the path between Two empty blue circles (The above Figure depicts this.)

Case 2: If C, then we block the path between Two empty blue circles (The above Figure depicts this)

Case 3: If C and C’s descendants are not there, then we block the path between Two empty blue circles (The above Figure depicts this)

7.21 Bayesian Network: Inference Using Enumeration and Variable Elimination

7.21.1 Inference using enumeration

Using our current example, suppose we wish to compute the probability of the bomb did not work probability, given that both the police officer and engineer’s message:

P (A |p, e) = αP (A, p, e)

We need to sum over all the values of the hidden variables:

αP (A, p, e) = ∑T ∑F P (A, p, e, t, f)

It may be a slight abuse of notation. The lowercase p, e means the police message = true and the engineer’s message = true. The lowercase t, f means that we sum over all variables B and A values. So now we have a sum over entries from the full joint. We can retrieve these using our Bayesian network structure:

∑T ∑F (A, p, e, t, f) = ∑T ∑F P(A) P(f) P (t | A, f) P(p | t) P(e | t)

In general, sums of this form could take O (n2 n) time to compute. There may be n values to multiply together to compute each product term and up to O (2n) complete terms to sum up. We can save some computations by pushing the P’s inward as much as possible:

∑T ∑F (A, p, e, t, f) = P(A) ∑F P(f) ∑TP (t | A, f) P (p | t) P(e | t)

We must do this sum for A = true and B =false.

7.21.2 Variable elimination method

Here we introduce a variable elimination algorithm that will help us avoid the exact computations; it is a form of dynamic programming. We introduce tables called factors to help us do the bookkeeping. Initially, we will have one factor for each CPT term in our expression.

Figure 7.11 Example – Variable elimination method

The initial tables store the values from the CPTs that we will need throughout the query evaluation. For example, the factor for A, denoted fA(A), is a two-element vector because we will be computing both P (A, p, e) and P (¬A, p, e) (we will normalize at the end to compute P (A| p, e) and P (¬A | p, e)).

The subscript A names the factor, and the (A) means that the value of A varies in the factor. The factor for F is also a two-element vector fF(F) because we will need to sum out the variable F. The factor fT (T, A, F) requires 2 × 2 × 2 elements because we will need all combinations of values for T, A, and F in the expression. Moreover, even though P and E have parents in the network, the factors fP(T) and Fe (T) have only two elements because, throughout the expression, we are only interested in cases where Police’s message = true and the engineer’s message= is true.

There are two operations that we need to be able to perform on factors: multiplication and summing out. The multiplication operation is similar to a natural join on database tables, as opposed to a matrix multiplication or an element-by-element multiplication. The set of variables in the product of two factors is the union of the sets of variables in the operands. In the product factor, there will be one entry for every combination of values for variables in the resulting set. The value of each entry is the product of the entries in the original tables that match the values for all relevant variables. For example, consider the product fPE(T) = fP(T)fE(T):

The union of variables in the operands is T. The entry for True in the resulting table is the product of the entries where T= True in the operands. Now consider fTPE (T, A, F) = fT (T, A, F) fPE(T).

The entry for T= True, A = True, F = True in fTPE (T, A, F) is the product of corresponding entries from fT (T, A, F) where T = True, A = True, F = True and from fPE(T). where T = True (since A, F do not appear in fPE(T). In general, we have

f (X1, …, Xj, Y1, …, Yk, Z1 …, Zl) = f1(X1, …, Xj, Y1, …, Yk) f2(Y1, …, Yk, Z1, …, Zl) The factors f1 and f2 share common variables Y. If f1 has a total of j + k variables and f2 has k + l variables, then the product will have j + k + l variables. Each entry in the result will have all the variables X ∪ Y ∪ Z and the entry for a particular valuation of X, Y, and Z will be the product of the entry in f1 that has the same values for X, Y, and the entry in f2 that has the same values for Y, Z.

The other operation we need for factors is summing out. If, for example, we sum out T from fTPE (T, A, F), the resulting factor will be named f¬TPE (A, F) (with the ¬T in the subscript denoting the summed T values). There is one entry in f¬TPE (A, F) for each combination of values of A, F in fTPE (A, F); the value of the entry is the sum of all entries in fTPE (A, F) that have the same values of A, F (but different values of T):

We are now ready to compute the answer to our query:

α P(A) ∑F P(f) ∑TP (t|A, f) P(p|t) P(e|t)

= α fA(A) ∑F fF(F) ∑t fT (T, F, A) fp(T) fe(T)

= αfA(A) ∑F fF(F) ∑t fT (T, F, A) fPE(T)

= αfA(A) ∑F fF(F) ∑t fTPE (T, F, A)

= αfA(A) ∑F fF(F)f¬TPE (F, A)

= αfA(A) ∑F fF¬TPE (F, A)

= αfA(A)f¬F¬TPE (A)

= αfA¬F¬TPE (A)

Finally, we have reduced many repeated variables from these calculations.

We write the repeated computations in a dynamic programming approach.

7.22 Bayesian Network: Rejection Sampling

So far, we have seen the syntax and semantics of the Bayesian networks. Also, we have seen the inference algorithms like variable reduction. Now it is time to generate the approximate inference methods, which will help us to get the exact inferences in large. We also need to multiply the connected networks when required. Multiple sample collections will make approximate inferences. From now on, we can see the sampling applied to the computation of posterior probabilities.

Suppose we toss a coin whose probability value is unknown. We do not know the exact probability for heads. How could we estimate this? At what number of trials will we ensure the correct probability?

To solve this, generate multiple samples. Even from Bayesian networks, we must generate huge samples as huge inferences. However, the samples should be unbiased. Usually, the Bayesian network is a generative model. Therefore, we can quickly generate samples from the distribution represented by Bayesian networks. Generate one variable at a time in topological order.

Rejection sampling is the general method for producing samples. This one is converting the complex samples to easy samples. Initially, it generates samples from the prior distribution. Then reject all those that do not match the evidence.

For example, in real-time, if we need to sample the count of fruit lovers given mangos as a piece of evidence or condition, we need to reject all other fruit lovers while conducting the sampling.

Rejection Sampling is very similar to the estimation of conditioning probabilities directly from the real world.

One more example: If we need to get the probabilities for dogs, the condition is tiny. We can call the probability P (Dog/Tiny = true). Here we can count how many dogs are in the street with a tiny appearance by ignoring all the big dogs.

While sampling from the Bayesian network, we can ignore the sampling, which is not required.

7.23 Bayesian Network: Likelihood Weighting

Instead of creating and rejecting a sample, it is possible to mix sampling with inference to reason about the probability that we reject the samples. In Likelihood weighting methods, each sample has a weight, and we compute the average using the weighted average of samples. Likelihood weighting is a form of weighted sampling where we sample the variables in the order defined by a belief network, and evidence will update the weights. The weights reflect the probability that it will not reject the sample.

Here each sample agrees with the evidence and pays some price for the agreement (weight). Here sample only non-evidence variables and weight each sample by the likelihood of evidence.

Examples (From our example case):

Table 7.6 Example – Likelihood weighting

P(a/p) = weight of samples with A=a / total weights of samples

Instead of rejecting all, create the weights for all combinations and get the total weights. Whatever we need, that particular condition will get divided by total weights.

7.24 Bayesian Network: Maximum Likelihood

We have been discussing more about Bayesian networks so far. Now we need to know how we write CPTs. How did the learning happen? How did we learn all these probabilities?

We take our example. Is it possible to learn from prior experience (Experience from previous terrorist attempts)? Yes. That is possible.

Several times, the agent’s engineer sent the right messages. How credible is he?

What is the probability of the quality of the bomb from experience?

How many times have we seen that the terrorist usually quits the plan?

We can get the count from previous experience and get the maximum likelihood.

After collecting all prior knowledge counts, we need to smooth these data in conditional probability cases. Smoothing will look at the below case.

From prior experience, we have seen that 50 out of 100 times, the terrorist escaped due to escape motivation; we need to smooth the data with the current situation’s importance.

The engineer will only send the right message in this situation even though he has a false record in the past.

Maximum likelihoods:

Find parameters that maximize the probability of seeing the data.

Then get the argmax (the maximum one) → argmax θ P (data | θ)

Easy to compute (Just via counting the experience)

We need to assume a uniform prior probability.

We need to create the distribution of the data.

We can also get an idea about maximum likelihood from a statistics point of view.

Imagine we have collected the statical data for the height of the students in one class. It is mentioned in Figure 7.12.

Figure 7.12 Example – Maximum Likelihood Procedure

In that class, the height of students varies from low to high, as per Figure 7.12 above.

Any dataset will fit under some probability-based random variable distribution. For example, it is mentioned in Figure 7.13.

Figure 7.13 Example – Maximum Likelihood Procedure

Figure 7.13 mentions that the entire dataset may fit under the normal distribution. However, sometimes it may fit either in the exponential or Gamma distributions.

Now assume that our data is in a normal distribution.

Figure 7.14 Example – Maximum Likelihood Procedure

The average height in the class fits precisely in Figure 7.14 above. It is in the centre. Here, the mean will be in the centre of the data. Therefore, normal distribution’s standard deviation 1 has high weightage. Please refer statistical section of our book (Chapters 11-15) for more details.

The measurements should be symmetrical around the mean. Sometimes this symmetrical is not possible with normal distribution. That is ok. It comes under different shapes like skinny, medium, and large bonded.

Figure 7.15 Example – Maximum Likelihood Procedure

We refer to Figure 7.15 above. As per that, the normal distribution may have any shapes. So, the shapes are not only bell shapes.

Figure 7.16 Example – Maximum Likelihood procedure

If we generate the normal distribution with the mean in the edge, so much data will reside too far from the mean. As in the above Figure 7.16, we can easily find out that.

Figure 7.17 Example – Maximum Likelihood Procedure

We may have many options if we consider so many varieties of normal distributions, as in Figure 7.17 above. However, the likelihood of observing all the weights could be higher.

Likelihood of observing data: If we see Figure 7.18, we can understand that the all-normal distributions will have different experiences in spreading the data from the mean. We need to get the maximum likelihood from this distribution. If we observe Figure 7.18 below, it clearly shows that one of the “N” normal distribution curves fits under maximum likelihoods. This one, we need to Figure it out.

Figure 7.18 Example – Maximum Likelihood Procedure

In Bayesian networks, we use maximum likelihood for parameter selection. Therefore, we choose the maximum likelihood parameter.

The value of Theta that maximizes this expression gives the maximum – likelihood hypothesis. The log-likelihood obtains the exact value.

What the maximum likelihood algorithm says means we must give equal priority to all the parameter options like 0.1, 0.2, 0.3, 0.5, 0.9 before we toss the coin. Give equally likely (Respect to all options) and measure the likelihood. Say we tossed the coin 1000 times. We may fall into any of the below options.

  • 0.1 times got head – 10% (100 out of 1000)

  • 0.2 times got head – 20% (200 out of 1000)

  • 0.55 times got head – 55% (550 out of 1000)

  • 0.7 times got head – 70& (700 out of 1000)

Irrespective of the parameter’s nature, we choose the best parameter as likelihood. Here 0.7 is the learning probability for heads based on the maximum likelihood calculation.

7.25 Bayesian Network: Maximum a Posteriori (MAP) Learning

Maximum A posteriori (MAP) involves calculating a conditional probability of observing the data given a model weighted by a prior probability or belief about the model.

MAP provides an alternate probability framework to maximum likelihood estimation for machine learning and artificial intelligence Bayesian learning.

Choose the parameter θ that maximizes

argmax θ P (θ | data) = P (data | θ) P(θ)

Find parameters that maximize the probability of seeing the data.

Allows the user to input additional domain knowledge via P(θ) –Prior probability assumption.

For example, before we toss the coin, we know the probability of heads is 0.5. It is prior probability.

What the maximum likelihood algorithm says means we must give equal priority to all the parameter options like 0.1, 0.2, 0.3, 0.5, 0.9 before we toss the coin. Give equally likely (Respect to all options) and measure the likelihood. Say we tossed the coin 1000 times. We may fall into any of the below options.

  • 0.1 times got head – 10% (100 out of 1000)

  • 0.2 times got head – 20% (200 out of 1000)

  • 0.55 times got head – 55% (550 out of 1000)

  • 0.7 times got head – 70& (700 out of 1000)

Irrespective of the parameter’s nature, we choose the best parameter as likelihood. Here 0.7 is the learning probability for heads based on the maximum likelihood calculation.

However, MAP tells us to add our prior intuition. Do not give equal weightage to all the parameters. Instead, tell the algorithm that we already know the probability of heads is 0.5. Then, we multiply this value by the likelihood of getting an accurate and maximum posterior report.

Say choose P(θ) – 0.5 for heads

Now P (θ | data) = 0.4 * 0.5 (assume Likelihood is 0.4). for 0.5

From these values, get the maximum value (Argmax)

It will produce better parameters.

When unlimited data is available, MAP is the best choice for learning.

08

Artificial Intelligence: Top View – Agent and Environments





Whatever we have seen so far is related to Artificial intelligence’s logic and algorithms. When we add all the logic together or all logic as features of the system, we get the whole system’s point of view.

In general, we need to discuss intelligent agents. If we recall chapter two, we have seen that the agent should follow rationality. The individual agent is an independent entity that works separately with given intelligence power. It means there are wide varieties of agents and huge characteristics of agents. We are going to deal with these varieties and characteristics in this chapter.

8.1 Agent in General

Before we discuss varieties and characteristics, we will generally see the agent’s nature. Refer to Figure 8.1. The agent must always have sensors. The sensor will perceive all the inputs from the environment. We can imagine our human body. As per the human body, the eyes and nose are sensors. Those are the input medium.

Once we perceive inputs from the environment through the sensor, the next step is decision-making. Agent must follow the agent rules. Or simply the rational functions. We can imagine that the agent is inserting the program library. Therefore, the agent follows these library rules. In our human body, this is happening via our brain. We program our brains. Whatever we have seen through our eyes, the brain will intelligently analyse before execution.

Finally, we execute the execution plan with the help of actuators [executors]. However, the agent program plans the instructions, and the executors of the agent follow the order. In our human body, the executors are our hands and legs.

In the overall picture, imagine this example. We are going to the dining table. The eyes see the food on the table and convey the picture to the brain. Brian perceived the visual input and planned to eat food. It is just simply generating the steps to eat food (1. The hand should go and take the small byte of the food 2. The hand should hand over food to mouth 3. The mouth should open the food 4. Teeth should grain the food 5. The tongue should send the grained food to the stomach). Here the brain provides many instructions. Actuators or executors follow the instructions. Here actuators are hands, mouth, and teeth.

Figure 8.1 Agent in general

8.2 Task Environment and PEAS Description

If we recall our dining table example, we know who are our actuators and sensors. In addition, any agent program or agent type will face some specific task environment. We denote this task environment of the agent type through the PEAS description.

What are PEAS?

P denotes performance

E denotes environment

A denotes Actuators

S denotes Sensors

So, any designed agent type mainly focuses on the abovementioned four elements. For example, imagine that the agent has been designed especially for some tasks. In that case, we focus their target elements on solving that task. For example, imagine this. We are working as artificial intelligent scientists and planning to design the agent for fruit picking activity. The final agent will have the below elements in its design.

Performance measure: How do we measure the performance of the agent? The fruit-picking agent must follow 95% accuracy while picking the fruits. Also, it should pick at least 50 fruits per hour. Here the performance measure will be the rate of execution and accuracy.

The agent’s environment: We have designed this agent to pick fruits on the farm. So, the environment is a fruit farm.

Actuators or Executors: Actuators of this fruit-picking agent are attached to a long stick, which picks the fruits. Agent moving wheels are another actuator. It will help the agent move from tree to tree and from one location to another.

Sensors: Sensors of the fruit picking agent are camera-based eye vision.

As mentioned above, the PEAS Elements vary for all the intelligent agents (performance measures, environment, actuators, and sensors). Suppose we consider the previous example of food-eating human agents (In the medical field, doctors will do some experiments with human diagnostic systems with the help of humanoids). The food-eating agent has camera-based eye vision as sensors. Hands and mouth are basic actuators. The performance measure will be the time to finish eating the food and accuracy in the digestive system. The environment is a dining table.

We can take one software-based intelligence agent that is not visible to the eyes—an online interactive tutor (A software-based intelligent agent). The function of this agent is to teach software-related lessons online. The performance measure of this agent is the student’s final score. If all the students get good marks, this intelligent software agent will get succeeded. The environment refers to the set of students and an online portal. Actuators are computer-based screens and displays. Sensors are keyboard entries by students (For example, feedback, questions, and answers)

8.3 Properties of the Task Environment (Characteristics)

Fully observable environment and partially observable environment:

At a time, if the agent can observe the entire environment during execution, then these are fully observable environments. Otherwise, it is a partial one. For example, the entire chessboard will be visible when the agent plays chess (Chess playing robot). The agent can plan anywhere in the task environment because it is not bounded and visible to the eyes. Nevertheless, if we take the fruit-picking agent, it cannot see the entire farm simultaneously. It cannot see the entire tree. Many apples are hiding inside the leaves of the branches. These are not visible to the eyes until we open those leaves. So, this environment is partially observable. In specific environments, it is unobservable. Nothing is visible to the eyes in the environment.

Single-agent and multi-agent:

As we saw in adversarial search, multiple agents, like football-playing agents, are required to solve the problems in some instances. One single agent cannot play football. As a team, we need more players. We call this agent system cooperative multi-agent, and this environment is a cooperative multi-agent environment. Similarly, opposite agents will be there while playing games. In our exhibition hall example, imagine if the terrorist also is an intelligent agent (Instead of a human) and some other terrorist group has sent this robot as their representative. This multi-agent environment is called a competitive multi-agent environment. Apart from this environment, we mainly deal with single-agent systems, like car-driving agents. Two agents cannot drive the car. Why we did not pick the fruit picking agent example for a single agent means most farm owners may use multi-cooperative agents for fruit picking.

Deterministic and stochastic agents:

If the agent declares the outcome confidentially, the environment changes after the current actions, which are deterministic environments. Imagine the environment is fully observable, and we know what will happen after the agent’s actions within the environment. We know what impact the agent’s action will create within the environment. The agent is controlling the environment. It gets called deterministic confidence, which is possible in fully observable environments. For example, we can consider the food-eating agent. The entire environment is visible. No one is there in the room to join us for dinner. The food plate needs to be moving. No one is pouring more food. Now the agent confidently says that if we take two bytes of food, food quality will get reduced. That is the objective for the agent, and the agent deterministically conforms to the output and next set of actions.

In reverse, the agent cannot determine anything in a stochastic environment due to uncertainty and probability. Recall our fruit-picking agent. It is a partially observable environment. The agent guesses some hidden fruits (inside the leaves) and attempts to pick the fruit. There is a 50% chance that the fruit may fall. There is a 50% chance that the fruit will not get picked. We need to find out whether the fruit is inside that hidden location. Partially observable environments are stochastic ones. Sometimes, the deterministic level is zero. We cannot say anything about the attempt, which are called non-deterministic environments. Randomly we act, and the environment has zero visibility to the agent’s eyes.

Episodic and sequential agent environments:

Most of the time, the actual executions get divided into multiple parts. For example, if the farm owner says to complete the tree by the end of the day Today for the fruit picking agent, the agent divides the task by fruit count. Suppose it considers intermediate success when it gets one fruit. In that case, there is no relationship between one fruit-picking action with another. These are called episodic actions, and this environment is called episodic environment. For example, a robot may say we have finished 18 episodes and already got 18 apples in the basket. The next episode will not be dependent on the previous episodes.

In the opposite of episode logic, there are sequential agent environments. Say, for example, a chess-playing agent and car driving agent. While playing chess, the agent’s current move will affect the entire game and the entire moves. So, all the steps in chess are correlated, and the actions are sequential. So, we call this environment a sequential agent environment.

Static environment and dynamic environment:

If the environment is not changing while the agent is in action, this is called a static environment. Everything is staying the same. For example, imagine the robot is writing an exam. The exam has a fixed time. Someone gives the exam question paper to the agent. Even though the agent is not turning the second page of the question paper now, this is a static environment. The questions in the question paper will stay the same. After one hour of the agent’s test, the robot answered all the questions on the first page and turned to the second page. Even though it is a partially observable environment, the environment is static until the robot finishes the exam.

In contrast, we have dynamic environments where the environment changes while the intelligent agent is in action. The car-driving agent faces this. The road is the environment, which keeps changing while the agent is in action.

Discrete environment and continuous environment:

Fixed permutations and combinations in the environment will be there for perceptions and actions. The action decides these discrete sets in action. These are called discrete environments. For example, even though the move combination is huge in chess, it is a discrete set in a chess game. The perception of the chess board at a time is one combination state. However, this combination of coins is called one particular chessboard state. These states and player actions are limited to a discrete set.

Some environments are continuous; continuously moving environments while driving a car by car driving agent is called a continuous environment. Why can’t we say the images as discrete while moving the car? The set of photos is discrete, and the video is continuous. However, even if we get the photos while driving, we cannot fit them into a discrete set. Here steering wheel actions cannot fit with a discrete set. It is continuously changing according to the road.

Known environment and unknown environment:

In some environments, the agent knows what will happen for all the actions. This information would have been given by someone or by experienced activity. These environments are called known environments. Imagine fruit picking agent. The agent knows the outcome of the actions in the given environment while picking the fruit. The action set is limited, and the known outcomes are also limited.

Often, the agent will be in an unknown environment and needs to conduct some experience in actions. Initially, the agent will learn about the environment and decide on the optimal action plan.

From the above properties, we can clearly say that the worst case of the agent environment would be the below case:

If the environment is partially observable, and the agent count is many [Need to cooperate or exchange the information], stochastic [outcome is not determined], sequential [all sub-actions are interconnected], dynamic [The environment keeps changing], continuous [precepts and actions are continuously happening and not fit under discrete set] and unknown [new experiments should execute for optimal output analysis].

8.4 Agent Program Types

Agent program also differs from agent varieties. Intelligent agent designs with specific architecture, particular hardware elements (based on the requirements), and programs.

So as per the above design, the agent program matters a lot. However, agent program design also fits under a few categories. Therefore, this section will discuss a few agent program types below.

8.5 Simple Reflex Agents

Figure 8.2 Simple reflex agent in general

These agent programs are just superficial. It makes decisions based on the current situation, which got received from the environment. The action rule library will be there as a reference point. Based on the rules, the action will get decided. These rules are simple.

We will consider a straightforward example. Our food-eating agent program will have a few rules like this; if food completes on a plate, mark completion; if food is like water, drink it instead of eating; if it is hard, use a knife to cut it and use a fork for help;

The agent programs use the rules. First, the agent will collect the inputs from the eye camera. Then, based on the sensor, the agent identified the mutton food. Since this is hard food, we use a knife to cuts this. The sensed information will get updated by the program agent. Then, the program agent will fix the actions based on the rule book. Finally, actuators will perform based on the planned actions. Here the actuators are hands.

8.6 Model-Based Reflex Agents

Figure 8.3 Reflex agent with model [Model-based reflex agent]

The simple reflex agent has one major issue. When the agent perceives one input from the sensor, immediately, the agent program decides the action based on the rule book and acts based on the planned action. Then again, in the second step, one more input will get received and act based on the new sensing information. Independent activities are going on here. Sometimes, we need a dependency, and we must maintain the history of perceptions for a few moments to finish the activities. Especially in partial observability environments, this is essential. This agent program type is called a model-based reflex agent.

This model will keep track of the previous inputs as a state. The model maintains the knowledge. This model will maintain the history and update the state of action based on the new inputs from the sensor.

Two different pieces of knowledge will be encoded here while we work on actions in the state. One is the dynamic environment changes due to others in the state. Second, the changes are due to our actions within the state.

For example, when the agent drives the car, it is supposed to change the lane. The agent must track where the other cars are going on the road. In one frame [One sensor information], we cannot decide. We need to keep the percept history of images. [10 previous sensor images will make the agent decide how to change the lane]. The knowledge represents a state as a model.

Our local search (Chapter 4) and logic (Chapter 6) explain these models.

8.7 Goal-Based Agents

Figure 8.4 Reflex agent with model and goal [Goal-based agent]

Most of the time, agents’ goals also need to be added to the decision-making. We can recall chapters 3 to 5. All search-based agents perform to solve the goals. The agent needs to keep the goal in the agent program as an objective. We refer to this agent program while deciding at the current moment. We can choose any option that never finishes if we do not have a goal. For car-driving agents, the destination is the goal. The car-driving agent cannot perform any actions without a goal. However, these goals influence the actions at every step. Say, for example, the car must take the right. However, the right-side road is closed. As per the map, it must take a right, and the model also captured the current issue on the road. The model suggests the agent decides based on the knowledge about this road from where we started. Here agent also thinks about goals. We can also return to the same road and take the left per the goal. The model helped here, and goals also influenced decision-making.

8.8 Utility-Based Agents

Figure 8.5 Reflex agent with model and Utility [Utility-based agent]

The agent will finish the task at any path when the specific goals are available. For example, the agent may choose anyone if there are six possible ways to reach the goal. However, using optimal solutions matters a lot. Therefore, the performance measure should provide the most optimal cost of the path and execution. Utility-based agents keep track of the utility cost and assume the optimal solution in the plan. While making decisions in any step, this utility-based objective and goals will be in the analysis.

8.9 General Learning Agents

Figure 8.6 General learning agent

The learning agent is an exceptional agent program type that empathizes more with learning activities. We divide the agent program into four concept-based components. As specified in Figure 8.6, the most essential component is the learning element. The responsibility of this component is to improve performance. The second essential component is the performance element, responsible for selecting external actions. It is straightforward. As we have seen in the previous sections, the agent gets input from the sensors and acts through actuators. Here performance element is doing the same activity (The same as other agents). The learning agent is unique; it takes feedback from outside through the sensors about the agent. It also decided how to improve the agent activities and improve the utility.

The design of the performance element and learning element depend on each other. Say the performance element need an answer from the learning element, like how to learn the new activity. Learning elements will need answers from performance elements like what and all learning elements need to keep as performance measures for this learning.

The performance element will pass the knowledge to the learning element, and the learning element will push the details about required changes.

The last component is the problem generator. Problem generators will suggest a few actions based on the previous experiments. The problem generator is the trial element dedicated to improvement analysis in learning. It takes learning goals as input, tries multiple options in the actions, and finds the optimal action. Finally, it will report to the performance element.

We will see one example for the above topic. On the first day, the fruit-picking agent destroyed one branch by mistake. The responsible component is the performance element, which works on the ground. (Who perceives through senor, executes the agent program, and executes via actuators)

The farm security suddenly expresses his negative thoughts about the agent. Agents perceive this feedback via sensors and share it with the learning element. The learning element thinks that the current program design of the performance element is not good, and it changes based on the feedback. The performance element will accept the changes proposed by the learning element. The learning agent will get the current details as knowledge from the performance element daily.

The agent pushes the learning goals to the program generator in the other thread. The goals keep experimenting with specific stuff, like distinguishing between branches and fruit. Once the experiment completes, it will update the learning solutions directly to the performance element. First, however, the learning elements will provide learning goals. Then, the learning element sets the considered goals for the experiment, which is getting decided based on our feedback.

Sometimes, performance standards will get analysed via rewards. At the end of the day, if the farm owner does not appreciate the agent, the learning element will get informed. Per the performance standard, we go for negative rewards and changes in action as usual learning element will modify the program accordingly and update the changes to the performance element. Also, in the other thread, we will set the learning goals as perfection while picking the fruit. Then, the agent informs these goals to the problem generator, who does experiments again.

All these activities will get repeatedly performed in the learning agent program.

09

Artificial Intelligence: Ethics





9.1 Basics

If we see the contemporary world, the usage of artificial intelligence is very high, and the usage will increase in the future. We cannot avoid AI anymore. However, so many claims that we will get so many ethical issues in the future from Artificial intelligence because they see lots of unethical activities in the present world. We, all researchers and engineers of AI, must address all AI-related ethical issues. The entire community must create ethical rules that everyone should follow. We depict some of the current world’s issues below.

  • We usually prepare the AI system with the help of unfair data.

  • AI Design may have bias, and we can push this bias by ourselves.

  • AI can make adverse decisions based on the inventor. Someone can misuse AI concepts.

  • Even though jobs are turning around with AI, there will be questions about the accuracy of the current human work.

As mentioned above, there are so many ethical issues that everyone faces or predicts against AI.

9.2 Robustness of AI Systems

Accuracy matters a lot when we deal millions of dollars in business. Primarily machine learning is getting used in most business decisions. If the accuracy fails, the business may loss money. However, it is not only business. Imagine if the intelligent agent is in public issues. We provide a few significant responsibilities to AI systems. AI must handle with care and robustness.

9.3 Transparency of AI Systems

Humans must involve and understand all the outcomes of artificial intelligence and machine learning. For example, we have yet to learn why specific algorithms in deep learning (deep neural network-based learning) work properly and why it is not work in some models. So human interaction and understanding are significant. Nowadays, we have a set of processes in machine learning named explainable artificial intelligence (XAI) which covers all these process setups. Explainable artificial intelligence (XAI) is a set of processes and methods that allows human users to comprehend and trust the results and output created by machine learning.

A human can audit rules in an XAI to understand how likely the system is to generalize to future real-world data outside the test set.

People must identify if cheating occurs in the model learning process and outcomes.

The semantic parser must get used.

9.4 Data Bias

People teach bias to artificial intelligence, like all others. Most of us are biased, and we teach bias. However, sometimes we do not know what we are teaching. Say, for example, via logic and probabilistic reasoning, we may teach Artificial intelligence with few data biases like “All engineers are hardworking,” Stock market agents are rational.”

Race, Gender, and Religion bias are the most data bias in the existing software. Therefore, there must be some regularity to control the bias.

All Artificial Intelligence system models are not perfect. However, the models significantly influence social and economic ethics.

Fairness: We need to make sure and ask ourselves whether we are building a fair AI-ML system.

We must create a better AI system by increasing fairness.

Whatever the results we have received, is that fair enough? The fairness review is a must.

For example, when AI decides on particular stuff, we need to verify the degree of data bias and fairness. For example, if AI suggests some share to invest, we need to understand whether the model is making fair decisions.

9.5 Accountability for Ethics Issues

Accountability must be clear. There are many parties involved in AI system design and implementation. Likewise, so many parties are involved in machine learning training.

If we have an automated car, there is a designer who designed the car, an engineer who created the algorithms, and a trainer who trains the system.

The accountability must be clear with the processes that we create.

9.6 Data Privacy

It is not only AI systems. We must handle data privacy and create policies even in standard computation engines. We need to teach policies to AI systems so that the AI system will handle privacy without any issues.

9.7 Cyber Security with AI and ML Systems

We need cyber security in AI and ML because of hacking. ML systems are deployed on the internet too. AI also is connected via the internet. Therefore, we must handle cyber security in both AI and ML systems.

Statistical Methods: Statistics and Probability Basics





Statistics plays a vital role in both machine learning and artificial intelligence. The training and final production data in machine learning require massive statistical analyses. With statistics and probability, we can perform our machine learning algorithms. These are very important. In the Artificial Intelligence field, modern uncertainty analysis comes from probability analysis. Computer vision and other robotics-related analyses need statistics and probability. Initially, in the first chapter of this section, we can look into the basics of statistics and probability. Probability and statistics are helpful because it provides a framework for analysing uncertainty and variability.

10.1 Data and Data Visualization

Our life entirely depends on data bags. Data, Data, Data. Wherever we go, we are seeing a massive set of data. For example, Raja tries to understand how many eat nonveg in the city. It is an extensive set of data. This data collection is required for him because he needs to submit this data as a college project, which is part of his curriculum. One fine Sunday, he and his girlfriend Ruby are planning to discuss with a random sample of people in the city, and they are planning to collect statistics from them. It will help them to analyse data as part of their survey.

Initially, he goes to the petrol bunk to fill the petrol for his two-wheeler tank. One employee is coming there to serve them, and he is saying that the petrol rate is 110 Indian rupees. Raja’s girlfriend suggests skipping this petrol bunk because the rate is very high for her. The petrol bunk employee mentions that there are 120 petrol bunks in the city and the average petrol price for that day is 112. He also mentions that his price is the lowest in the city. Raja thinks about it, and his intelligence from his brain says that he can only consume petrol in this shop. He also accepts that, and the deal is over with that petrol bunk. However, his girlfriend says that this is a blind decision.

Raja says they do not have any relevant websites in the city that can provide details about the current petrol price information. Moreover, it takes time to go physically to all the petrol bunks and collect the price details for that day. So now his girlfriend is somehow convinced.

As mentioned above example, in real life, everything is data. We need data visualization for all the data dealt with in day-to-day life. Therefore, data visualization matters a lot. The collected possible data depicts using visualization form. These data presentation skills are essential for data analysis, and these data analyses are required to infer some crucial decisions.

For example, the pie chart below helps Raja’s girlfriend decide to purchase the right surprise gift for Raja’s birthday.

Figure 10.1 Example Pie Chart – Raja’s Interest

From the above pie chart (Figure 10.1), Raja’s girlfriend infers that movie tickets are the best choice for Raja’s birthday gift. So, she buys movie tickets for Raja. Likewise, we have bar graph tables, line graphs, Venn diagrams, and 2-way tables to present the data. All these graphs help us to infer the data.

10.2 Central Tendency

Now we want to start analysing the data set differently. This section will examine what we all measure of central tendency. Central tendency means the centre of the dataset—the average value of the given set of values.

10.3 Mean

“Mean” means average. Therefore, it is an arithmetic average or arithmetic mean. We usually use the below formula for calculation.

Here µ is the mean, and N is the number of data elements in the data set. In the numerator, we are adding all the data set arithmetic values. We can also say like below.

“µ is calculated from the total sum of all the data elements by dividing with a count of data elements.”

It is a balancing point. We can discuss this with one example. If we have datasets 7, 9, 11, 13, 15, the mean value will be 11. In the same way, if we take another data set with the values like 9, 11, 13, the mean value will be 11. It means the mean value is the centre value of all the data values.

10.4 Median

The Median is another form of centre value. We are not just simply taking average value here. We are giving more importance to several data values of the data set instead of their arithmetic value.

Say, for example, in a class, ten people are studying. Two people got 100 marks in the maths exam. Two got 90 marks, one got 50 marks, and five got 0 marks. When we calculate the mean, we get the value
below.

100+ 100 + 90+ 90+ 50+ 0+ 0+ 0+ 0+ 0 / 10 = 43

However, imagine if someone sees this mean value, they will say that this class is good, and the mean value of 43 guarantees that this school’s education is average and good. However, five people got zero marks. So, 5 out of 10 people got zero. This mean value does not show the exact middle representation of the data set because a few high values influence the mean value. The Median provides the average data value from the count perspective instead of an arithmetic average. See the below procedure for calculating the median value.

Convert the data values in ascending order.

For example, if we get the data set values like 90, 50, 100, 90, 0, 100, 0, 0, 0, 0 → We need to keep this dataset in ascending order like 0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 50, 90, 90, 100. However, even descending order also is acceptable.

  • From the total count, get the centre value by using the formula (n+1) /2

    • Here in our case, the n value is 10, so the centre data value will be (10 + 1) /2 =5.5

    • What does this mean? Get the middle data position of the data set. Keep in mind that this is not the actual value of the dataset. It is the position value.

    • If we have an odd number, the middle data-position will have an exact number instead of a decimal value. In case we have nine values in the data set, then the middle data-position will be (9+ 1)/ 2= 5

  • Now, this is the final step.

    • Suppose our middle data position is an integer; the median value is in the same data position as the ascending order.

    • If our middle data position is decimal, the median value is between two numbers, surrounded by this data-position value.

    • In our data set, the middle position value is 5.5. it means we need to consider both the data position values 5 and 6. Now average these two values.

    • The fifth and sixth position values are 0, 50. If we average them, the median value is 25.

    • So, the median value for the above example is 25. However, if we compare it with the mean, the mean value is 43.

Now we will take one more odd number data set as an example. The dataset values are 45, 34, 2, 130, –13

  • Step 1: Keep the data in ascending order: –13, 2, 34, 45, 130

  • Step 2: Middle data-position value is: (5+1)/2 = 3

  • Step 3: Since this is an odd dataset, get the third data value from the ascending order set. It is the Median of the dataset. The value is 34.

10.5 Mode

When there is a repeated occurrence of the same data values multiple times in the data set, we must address that. So, the mode is the central tendency’s variety, empathizing with these multiple occurrences. It is called mode. It is like an election result. Whoever gets more occurrences in the election (By vote) will be the top person. Similarly, the most repeated data value is the mode of the dataset.

Suppose we have a dataset like 56, 67, –3, 0, 100, 0, 34, 8, 0, 0, 0; then the mode value is 0 because the value repeats four times. So, in the class, if multiple people got zero marks in the exam, then the class mode is zero.

Sometimes, two people get equal votes. The sample data set is this: 12, 12, 23, 23, 67, 0, 100. Here 12 also appears twice, and 23 also appears twice. We need to consider both these values here, and this data set is called the bi-model dataset.

Suppose we have three or more data values with equal data appearance count. In that case, this data set is the only referred dataset.

10.6 Measures of Spread

Whatever we have seen in central tendency only refers to centre values. However, we need to analyse the dataset from a spread point of view. The places where the most spread is going on and where less spread is going on will get analysed. There will be a cluster in the dataset, and we can analyse this with the help of spread. We also call these scatters and spread measures.

There are three types of measures of spread, which we will see in this section.

  • Range

  • Interquartile range (IQR)

  • Variance and standard deviation

10.7 Range

The number system is infinite. However, any data set will have a limited and finite range within this infinite number system. We need to calculate this difference by separating the most significant value of the dataset from the lowest value of the dataset. It is called range. If we take the below dataset, the range value is 50.

34, 84, 45, 81, 50

The most significant value is 84, and the smallest value is 34. So, the range is 84 – 34 = 50

10.8 Interquartile Range (IQR)

If we divide anything into two parts and then divide both parts into another two parts, we get four parts of the entire element. These parts are quarters. Similarly, we can divide any dataset into four parts.

We need to divide the ascending-order dataset into four parts. Even though we virtually cut them by four parts, we get three intermediate positions. These three intermediate positions are called quartiles (Quartile 1 is in the 25th percentile, Quartile 2 is in the 50th percentile, and Quartile 3 is in the 75th percentile).

Here the interquartile range (IQR) is the difference between the Median of the upper half and the Median of the lower half. It means that IQR = value in Quartile 3 – value in Quertile1. We will see this procedure with one example dataset.

The dataset is this [Here, we perform the Ascending order]

1, 1, 1, 1, 2, 3, 235, 8000

If we divide these into three quartiles, we get

Here IQR will be Q3 – Q1; we need to get the Q3 value. Q3 is actually 2 + 235 /2 = 118.5

Q1 is actually (1+1) / 2 = 1.

Now IQR = Q3 – Q1 = 118.5 – 1 = 117.5

It is straightforward. For any given dataset, get the median value. This median value is Q2. Then get the Q1 and Q3 values from the two-sub-division dataset by calculating the Median of the sub-divided datasets.

10.9 Data Set Value Changes and Outliers

When the entire dataset values get changed, what will happen to the central tendency and spread of the particular dataset? There are two possibilities in the data set changes

If we change all the values by adding one standard constant value or subtracting one typical constant value.

If we add one additional data value into the data set or remove one data value from the data set

Will see these two changes below:

10.10 Constant Addition or Subtraction

We will check the impact of constant addition or subtraction in the below example. Imagine we have a data set like below

1, 1, 1, 1, 2

If we calculate all the centre tendency and spread values, we get the below values.

  • The mean is 1

  • The Median is. 1.

  • The mode is. 1.

  • The range is 2 – 1 =0

  • The IQR is 1 – 1 = 0

Suppose If we add the constant value 100 to these datasets, we get the new dataset like below

100, 100, 100, 100, 102

We get the below values if we calculate all the centre tendency and spread values for the new dataset.

  • The mean is 100.4

  • The Median is. 100

  • The mode is. 100

  • The ranges 2

  • The IQR is 0

When we add or subtract the constant, the Mean, Median, and Mode value change. However, Range and IQR stay the same. It means the spread will not get changed. It applies to any data set.

Add new value into a dataset or remove data value from the data set

Adding or removing the data value will affect the balancing point for the mean value. For example, adding or removing a data value from one side of the mean will force us to move the mean if we want to stay balanced.

What will happen to the Median: When we add or remove data value in the data set, sometimes the Median will change. It depends on the value with which we are working.

10.11 Extreme Values

The number having massive power to change the data set mean is called the outlier. It is a number on the dataset’s extreme upper or lower ends. The outlier means extreme values which are not close to the cluster. It is also a highly odd value.

For example, if we consider the data set 1, 1, 1, 1, 8035

While seeing the data set itself, we can quickly identify the outlier. There are six values in the above dataset. In that, five are near some cluster. One value is extreme. So, this extreme value is an outlier.

We can take one more example. Say we have a data set 1, 3, 5, 9, 24, 8045

In this example, the fifth value is more significant than the first four. However, the sixth value is significantly higher. The outliers will be in the extreme left or extreme right. So, 8045 is the outlier in the above example.

In the real-time example, the stock market value will peak some days. Extreme peak (Extreme up or extreme down) will be odd in 365-day average calculations. That exceptional day value is a special moon day, which we must avoid in average calculations. Otherwise, we will not see the accurate value. These values are outliers.

10.12 Box and Whisker Plots

Box and whisker plots are an excellent way to represent the data set. It will help us to show the Median and spread of the data set at the same time.

Generally, a box and whisker plot will look like the one below.

Figure 10.2 Box and Whisker plot example

The big rectangular in the centre is the box in Figure 10.2 above. The little lines extending out from the sides are the whiskers.

We can identify the Median, range, and upper and lower bounds while seeing it. So, the IQR is just a simple calculation.

Another representation is five number summaries. Five-number summary means just showing the five values from the dataset: the minimum value, Q1 value, Median value, Q3 value, and maximum value. In one shot, we can show these data sets. Hence, these are called five-number summaries. Suppose we take an example dataset as 1, 2, 3, 4, 5. Then here, the minimum value is 1, Q1 is 2, Median is 3, Q3 is 4, and the max value is 5.

10.13 Sample Mean, Variance, and Standard Deviation

10.13.1 Sample Mean

In this section, we will discuss the population and sample. In the real world or the actual case, we cannot take all the data values for calculations. For example, suppose Raja would like to understand how many men are going to the gym in the whole country. In that case, it is impossible because this country’s population is 1.3
billion.

So always collecting the population data is impossible. In many cases, we need to go for sampling data. Instead of discussing with all 1.3 billion people, we can discuss with only 200 people. They are the sample of that population.

So, collecting the data from the sample is very easy. However, we need to ensure that this sample represents the population crowd. Therefore, we need to collect the random value innovatively. We will discuss more about this in the later sections. It means that we will discuss more about how we collect the data. However, now we will understand the difference between the population and the sample.

How will the mean get affected for both population and sample data? For example, collecting data from 200 men about their sleeping hours will only be a small part of the population. For example, if we get the mean of all the 200 people as 7 hours, it will not be accurate to the population mean. Here, how we collect the sample also matters. If we collect the proper population spread as a sample, this sample will be correct.

So, in the statistics world, we donate the mean in two different ways. One is the population means, and the other is the sample mean. The “Population mean” gets denoted as µ, and the “sample mean” gets denoted as .

10.13.2 Variance

Variance measures how far the data is getting spread from the mean. For example, population variance depicts as by σ2.

Why do we mention population variance as σ2? We will come to know about this at the end of this section. Before discussing variance, we will first see the variance formula. The formula for the population variance is this.

In the above example, µ is a population mean. When we say (xi – µ), it is the distance between µ and other points. How is the deviation getting spread at each point? Then (xi – µ)2 is the squared deviation. Why do we need squared deviation?

The deviation is always getting spread in both positive and negative lines. It means that the mean is in the middle. The left side of the mean always gets negative values when we calculate the mean (xi – µ), and the right side always gets positive values when we calculate the mean (xi – µ). We add these negative and positive values together and get zero as a deviation result. It will happen all the time. Then here, how do we visualize the spread? It is the reason we square the variance. When we square the deviation, we get a positive number. When we square the negative values, we get a positive number. The positive deviation values will get a positive number when we square them. So, we add both positive values and get the total positive number as squared deviation.

Calculating the sample variance is also a similar process to population variance. We have one minor change in it.

As the sample mean, the squared deviation also uses a small “n” value. We also call this a sample value. The denominator “n” refers to the sample count. The researchers also found that the above sample mean formula needs to be corrected. As per the research theorem, we must add degrees of freedom in the above formula. Instead of dividing the count by “n” values, we need to use “n-1” values. This n-1 will provide the accuracy value between the sample and population mean. We will discuss this degree of freedom algorithm sometimes nearby in this book. Otherwise, we will cover it later. We can understand that the sample deviation needs to be divided by n-1. So that one last number we keep as the degree of freedom. That DOF’s actual deviation will depend on the other n-1 values compared to population deviation. It is sufficient now. There is a theorem and proof for this, and that theorem is interesting. We call this n-1 formula an unbiased standard deviation. The previous one is a biased deviation. However, this unbiased variance provides accurate results in sample variance.

Refer to the below example, which refers to variance.

For example, consider this data set 1, 2, 3, 4, 5. The mean of the data is 3, and the variance of this data set will look like below.

Table 11.1 Variance calculation sample

So S2 value will be 10/5 = 2. [Population dataset]

If these are sample datasets, the sample variance will be 10/4 = 2.5

10.13.3 Standard Deviation

Standard deviation measures how much data in a dataset varies from the mean. The larger the standard deviation value, the more the data in the set varies from the mean. Conversely, the smaller the standard deviation value, the less the data in the set varies from the mean.

Population standard deviation denotes as σ, and the value is the root of the population variance value (σ2). The root of population variance will provide a population standard deviation. Similarly, the sample standard deviation will get calculated from sample variance S2, and we denote this as S. Just the root of sample variance (S2) will provide the sample standard deviation S value.

10.18 Normal Distribution

We already mentioned Normal distribution in the previous section. It is a bell-shaped curve where the mean and Median are always equal. The spread also is in a bell-shaped curve. The spread is smooth, and all options will be available in this distribution. If we take an example of any actual distribution in life, it will usually be a normal distribution. Consider any school or college classroom. All categories of rank holders will be there in the mixed classrooms. There will be few poor performers, and there will be few top performers in the classroom. Most of the people will be in the middle of the distribution too. The normal standard distribution curve has been visualized below in Figure 10.11.

Figure 10.11 Normal distribution

We will discuss the essential properties of normal distribution in the below section. In statistics and probability, the normal distribution is significant. The normal distribution also has an empirical rule. It is called the 68-95-99.7 rule. The rule tells us that a 68% chance of data points falls within “One” standard deviation of the mean in the normal distribution. 95% chance of data point falls within “Two” standard deviations of the mean. Moreover, a 99.7% chance of data point falls within “Three” standard deviations of the mean. Graphically if we want to see this distribution percentage in the normal distribution, refer the below Figure 10.12

Figure 10.12 Normal distribution with SD distributions
