That information is specified below. Here “One,” “Two,” and “Three” standard deviations are spread on both sides with an equal percentage split.

Figure 10.13 Normal distribution with SD distributions

We deal with percentile a lot with normal distribution. What is a percentile, and how will it be used in a normal distribution?

The percentile is significant because, in statistics, we will refer to this percentile everywhere. The nth percentile is the value such that the ‘n’ percent of the values lie below this range. Conversely, a value in the 95th percentile is greater than 95% of the data. The Median is always the 50th percentile mark. The percentile at the rightmost corner is 100%, and the leftmost corner is 0%. Now we understand what the percentile concept is. Refer to below Figure 10.14 for percentile visualizations.

10.19 Z-Score

The Z-score helps us identify the specific point’s standard deviation from the mean in normal distributions. We need to know this data while working on the analysis. For example, we have one particular point in normal data distribution and need to know the percentile of the data set, which falls under this point. Also, we want to know how much percentile of the data set falls above these points. To know this percentile, we need a z-score of that point. We will discuss in the next paragraph why we need a z-score for percentile data. At present, understand this z-score value. Then, we use the formula to calculate a z-score for normally distributed data (normal distributions).

Here x means our data point where we need a standard deviation z-score. μ is the mean, and σ is the standard deviation. Z-score is simply calculating how far this point is from the mean.

We usually subtract the mean from our data point and divide that answer by the standard deviation of that point. Here we will get a z-score. We have z-tables for reference. We refer to this table for any z-score value, which will provide the percentile data. It will say how much percentile data is below this z-score point. We have two z-tables. One is a negative z-score table, and the other is a positive z-score table. Negative table means which falls left side of the mean because any data point will be lesser than the mean. A positive table means it falls right side of the mean because any data point will be greater than the mean.

Refer to the below Figure 10.15. If our z-score value is 1.96, we can look at this table for a 1.96 z-score value. The value that’s shown here is the percentile. So, the 97.5th percentile data set falls under this z-score. So, the right side will be 100-97.5 = 2.5% [The percentage data above this z-score point]

Figure 10.15 Z-score Values

To find the percentage of data above our data point, we have to subtract the z-score’s value from the hundred.

10.20 Probability

Now it is a time to discuss more about probability. Probability is significant in Mathematics. It is getting used in several logics and algorithms. In Artificial Intelligence and Machine learning, probability plays an important role.

What is probability? Probability is how likely it is that something will occur. We will write the probability in fractions, decimals, or any percentage form. However, the probabilities are always between zero and one.

The probability of zero means a 0% chance of that occurrence in that event. The probability of 1 means there is a 100% chance that it will happen. A probability between 0.5 and 1 means it is likely to occur. A probability of 0.5 is fascinating and has an equal chance for that event. Typically, in probability classes, mathematicians discuss flipping a coin. Alternatively, they will talk about rolling the dice. Otherwise, they will talk about pulling the card from the playing cards. In general, everyone uses these three examples.

How likely will we get heads when we flip a coin?

Everyone knows that we always get a 50% chance to get heads while flipping the fair coin.

How likely will the queen be there when pulling a card from the deck? How likely will things happen as compared to other neighbour events?

Here we usually denote the probability of the event as P of the event. So, we can say P(event). We refer to the probability of getting heads as P(Heads) and the probability of getting tails as P(Tails). We refer to the probability that a queen is paying cards as P(Queen).

Simple Probability: We must check the probability against the total possible outcomes.

P(event) = Outcomes that meet our case / all possible outcomes

Here all possible outcomes mean sample space. It is the entire world of possibilities. For the flipping coin example, the sample space is heads or tails. The sample space is getting denoted as

S = {H, T}

So, only two possible outcomes are there. When we try to calculate heads alone for probability measurement, we get a probability like the one below.

P (Heads) = ½. There is a 50% chance that we will get heads when we flip a coin one time.

We can also denote the actual outcomes as all possible equally likely outcomes.

P(event) = Outcomes that meet our criteria/All possible equally likely outcome

Example: The probability of pulling a queen card from the total possible 52 cards in the playing cards game will be like below

P(Queen) = 4/52 = 1/13 (4 different flowers of the queen will be there)

The probability also has two flavours.

Theoretical probability: We know the probability before executing any experiment. It is called Theoretical probability.

Experimental probability: The reasonable probability, and this is getting calculated from the recent experiment.

Example: Theoretically, when we flip the fair coin, the probability of getting “Heads” will be 0.5 (½). In the experiment, we may get heads when we flip the coin. From the experiment, the probability value will be 100% for the event that just happened. When we keep conducting experiments, after five experiments, we get the outcomes like this: H, H, H, T, H.

Here the experimental probability of heads will be 4/5 = 80%. Usually, the experimental probability will come closer to the theory after massive trials. Slowly the accuracy will come after an extended period, like 1 million trials. However, it differs between cases.

10.21 Probability – Addition, Union, and Intersection

Most of the time, we must find the probability from two different events. It is because two different events occur together within one experiment. Event is the specific collection of outcomes from the sample space. For example, what is the probability of getting the sum value as an actual value when we die a pair of dice?

There are 36 possibilities as the total outcomes when we die a pair of dice. Out of these 36 possibilities, we have 18 getting even sum values.

The below specified Thirty-six possibilities are total outcomes. In that, 18 values refer to even sum values (when we add two dice values, we get the sum).

((1,1), (1,2), (1,3), (1,4), (1,5), (1,6),
(2,1), (2,2), (2,3), (2,4), (2,5), (2,6),
(3,1), (3,2), (3,3), (3,4), (3,5), (3,6),
(4,1), (4,2), (4,3), (4,4), (4,5), (4,6),
(5,1), (5,2), (5,3), (5,4), (5,5), (5,6),
(6,1), (6,2), (6,3), (6,4), (6,5), (6,6))

We can draw in Venn diagram if we have two different events from the same sample space. The total space has 36 possibilities. However, adding two events within the same space might have some relationships. We can see those relationships here.

For example,

Event 1 → when we analyse the probability of getting the sum value as an even value, we get 18 possibilities.

18/36

Event 2 → when we analyse the probability of getting one from either of the dice, we get 11 possibilities.

11/36

Figure 10.16 Probability addition

The above Venn diagram (Figure 10:16) shows both events. These events are mutually exclusive. Mutually exclusive means both events get the same outcome. For example, event one also gets a (1,1) outcome. When we say sum value as even, the same value pair (1,1) will fit under this event two probability. These are called mutually exclusive.

Addition rule

Probability addition is constantly coming under the addition rule or the sum rule of the probability that

P (A or B) = P(A) + P(B) − P (A and B)

If there is no overlap between A and B, then we call those events mutually exclusive. Then P (A and B) will be 0.

Now what happens if there is no overlap between A and B? In that case, A and B are mutually exclusive (or disjoint), and P (A and B) will be 0. It means the addition rule will simplify this way: P (A or B) = P(A) + P(B) − P (A and B)

P (A or B) = P(A) + P(B) − 0
P (A or B) = P(A) + P(B)

Figure 10.17 Probability – Mutually exclusive events

Union and intersection

In the addition rule, we have seen that we have “or” and “and” keywords, which can represent “∪”, “∩” These are called unions and intersections.

P (A ∪ B) = P(A) + P(B) − P (A ∩ B)

P (A ∪ B) is called the union of A and B; (Add both sets of outcomes here. It is the addition.)

P (A ∩ B) is called the intersection of A and B; (Both A and B will have the outcomes)

11

Statistical Methods: Independent probability





When we say independent events, which do not affect one another, for example, if we toss the coin two times, the results of the first toss do not affect the second toss. So, these tosses are independent events.

If we get a head on the first toss, the second toss is equally likely. So, the second event has a chance for heads or tails. It is independent of the first event results. So, these are called independent probability or independent events.

11.1 The Multiplication Rule

If we want to find the probability of multiple independent events or the joint occurrence, we multiply the probabilities. It is called the multiplication rule. The probability that we get heads twice when we flip a coin.

P (A and B) = P(A) ⋅ P(B)

The formula mentioned above is the multiplication rule. We can multiply two independent events (which are sequential) to find the total probability.

We can take a simple example of this. The probability of getting heads in the first coin toss is 1/2. The probability of getting heads in the second toss also is 1/2. There are independent events.

The results of the first toss do not affect the other toss events. Therefore, we can calculate the probability of getting two heads from the two independent events by multiplying the individual probabilities to see the overall probability of the two events. For the above example, the total probability will be ¼. We got this from the multiplication rule.

(1/2) * (1/2) = (1/4)

We have four options from two independent events. Those are {HH, HT, TH, TT}. From this, we have only one option when we need only heads from both events. That is {HH}. That is why we are getting (1/4) as the overall probability for getting “Heads” in both trails.

Here multiplication rule tells us that when we have an independent event multiple times, we can multiply all the events.

For example, the probability of getting king from 52 playing cards will be 1/13. Again and again, when we replaced the card, we picked it with card reck and tried the same experiment again; this comes under the independent probability category. So, we can multiply the probabilities.

If we try for five trails, we calculate the probability of all five trails like below.

= (1/13) * (1/13) * (1/13) * (1/13) * (1/13)

11.2 Dependent Probability

It is opposite to the independent probability. We call this dependent probability if the event entirely depends on one previous event. For example, suppose the second subsequent event depends on the first event’s outcome; this is called dependent probability. For example, we get the king card when we pull a card from 52 deck cards. Here imagine that we need to replace the card. Then the second subsequent event probability and outcome are ultimately affected by the first outcome because now we have only 51 cards and three kings out of 51 cards. So, when we calculate the probability for the king in the second attempt, we will get the probability as 3/51. So, this second probability is called conditional probability. We usually denote the conditional probability as P(A | B). So, we need to call this probability of A given B.

Using the multiplication rule, if we calculate the overall probability for two subsequent events of King cards under this dependent probability category / Conditional probability category, we can get the probability like below.

  • P (King from event 1) = 1/13

  • P (King from event 2 when we did not replace king from event 1) = 3/51=1/17

Then multiplication rule will be

(1/13). (1/17) = 1/221

In general, this multiplication rule formula will be like below

P (A and B) = P(A) ⋅ P(B|A)

Here P(B|A) is the probability that B occurs given that A has already occurred. If we recall the activities again, imagine that we have already completed the first event. Then, we use this first event’s experimental outcome while calculating the second event. So, the second event is purely in the new condition world (i.e., within the outcome, our unlimited options got reduced for the second event. so, the total outcomes got reduced due to the first outcome).

11.3 Bayes’ Theorem

This theorem tells us the probability of an event, given prior knowledge of related events that occurred earlier. Bayes’ Theorem is

P(A|B) = P(B|A) ⋅ P(A) / P(B)

Bayes’ theorem is an excellent tool for identifying conditional probability with prior knowledge and new evidence. We can also use the tree method, which is a simple one. The tree method can be used for minor problems and cannot work with the tree method on big problems. That is why Bayes’ theorem is a very effective one. We will look into one more example. In that example, we will solve the problem with both methods. Both methods will have the same answer.

Example:

Raja is buying one lottery sheet which belongs to one famous lottery system of that city. In that lottery sheet, one crucial secret number is on the backside. It has four flavours (1, 2, 3, 4). There are two lottery shops in the street. One is fair shop. Another shop is unfair. The fair shop’s lottery sheet will always have an equal probability of getting the four-flavoured secret number. It means we get a lottery sheet with a random number with a probability of (For 1 = ¼; For 2 = ¼; For 3= ¼; For 4=¼).

However, if anyone enters the unfair shop, 50% of the sheets have one as a secret number. So, it is a fake sheet. However, 50% of sheets are valid, and it has an equal probability of getting the other three secret numbers (2, 3, 4).

Raja needs to find out which shop is a fair shop and which is an unfair one. He has chosen one shop and bought one lottery sheet already. He got a secret number as 1. What is the probability that Raja chose the unfair shop?

Solution through Bayes’ theorem:

Since we are looking for the probability that the shop is unfair, given that the lottery sheet’s secret number is 1, we are looking for P (unfair shop /1). In this case, P(A)=P (unfair shop) and P(B)=p (1). So, we need to find the following values and put them into the formula

  • P (unfair shop /1)

  • P (unfair shop)

  • P (1)

We know from the problem that P (1/unfair shop) = ½, and since there are two shops in the street and each choice is equally likely P (unfair shop) = ½

The probability of getting one as a secret number is the probability of choosing the unfair shop and getting one as a secret number or the probability of choosing the fair shop and getting one as a secret
number.

Let us first find the probability that the secret number is from the unfair shop, and we get one as a secret number.

P (unfair shop) = 1/2, and the probability of a one on the unfair shop is 50 %. So, the probability of unfair shop and 1 is

P (unfair shop and 1) = (1/2) (1/2) = ¼

Now let us find the probability that the shop is fair, and we got one as a secret number.

P (Fair shop and 1) = (1/2) (1/4) =1/8

Therefore, the probability of getting one as the secret number is

P (1) = 1/4 + 1/8
P (1) = 2/8 + 1/8
P (1) =3/8

Putting these values into Bayes’ Theorem, we get

P(A|B) = P(B|A) ⋅ P(A) / P(B)
P(A/B) = ½. ½ / 3/8
P(A/B) = ¼ / 3/8
P(A/B) =1/4.8/3
P(A/B) =8/12 = 2/3

Solution through tree method:

How do we get this? Do we need to reconfirm from the tree view? Let us work on that now; we need to show branches in our tree for every event in our problem. Initially, Raja picked the shop. The shop can be fair or unfair, and we have an equal chance of picking each one.

Each shop can lead to four possible outcomes, so that we will show these in our tree. However, we also know that the unfair shop will land on one 50 % of the time and that all three other outcomes are equally likely, which means we could think of this as three possible outcomes leading to 1 and one possible outcome leading to each of the other numbers.

However, we always need to make sure our tree is balanced. We cannot have six branches from the unfair and four from the fair shop side. We have to have an equal number of branches on both sides so that we will scale up both sides to the least common multiple, 24. That means we need to scale up all the unfair shop branches by four and all the fair shop branches by 6.

The next part of the problem tells us that Raja has chosen one shop and got a 1. So, we will trim all the branches of our tree that lead to results that did not occur.

Now we want to know the probability that Raja chose [Given the unfair shop]. First, remember that each branch on the unfair shop side represents four possibilities, so 12 branches come from the unfair shop side. On the other hand, the branch on the fair shop side represents six possibilities, so six branches are coming from the fair shop side.

Therefore, the probability that Raja chose the unfair shop is all the outcomes coming from the unfair shop side, 12, divided by all branches in total, 18: P (unfair shop) = 12/ (12 + 6) = 2/3.

So, we matched Bayes’ theorem and standard tree methods. Both the values are the same. It is a simple example. If we are working on a big problem, imagine how helpful Bayes’ theorem is. It is very effective in Artificial Intelligence and Machine Learning.

12

Statistical Methods: Discrete Random Variables





12.1 Discrete Random Variables and Probability Distributions

A discrete random variable is a variable that can only take on discrete values.

What is discrete?

Any fixed set is discrete. The opposite of discrete is continuous. We cannot say that the finite option is continuous. The accurate height of a human being can be anything like the one below.

“5.1 feet,
5.111 feet,
5.11111 feet,
5.111111 feet,
5.11111111 feet,
5.111111111 feet”

Do we see the standard height in the above example? From accuracy, we can go any deep in height. However, when we say all the class members have a range of heights between 5 and 6.2, this is called a continuous dataset. So, we cannot make precisely finite the options in continuous.

Opposite to this, discrete will have an finite set. So, it can have either 1000 or 2 trillion data items. However, it is finite.

For example, if we flip a coin twice, we can only get heads zero times, one time, or two times. We cannot get heads 1.5 times or 0.31 times. The number of heads we can get takes on a discrete set of values: 0, 1, and 2.

In probability distributions for all random variables, the probabilities of each possibility have to sum to 1, or 100 %.

It means that flipping the coin three times will get eight possible outcomes (TTT, TTH, THT, THH, HTT, HTH, HHT, HHH)

Suppose we take only heads as random variables; then we will have a headcount option as 0 Heads, 1 Head, 2 Heads, and 3 Heads. If we add all these probabilities, The total will be 100%. It is an excellent discrete probability distribution.

  • Say 0 Heads = 1/8

  • 1 Heads = 4/8

  • 2 Heads = 2/8

  • 3 Heads = 1/8

  • So Total 1/8 + 4/8 + 2/8 + 1/8 = 1

The total should always have 100%, and the probability will be 1. So, any probability of the random variable will be between 0 and 1.

12.2 Additional use of Random Variables

In case we want to know the probability for three heads in the above example (In case we do not have that information), then other possible probabilities count will help us to decide because all the possible values in the random variable should be 1 (100%)

Say,

  • 0 Heads = 1/8

  • 1 Heads = 4/8

  • 2 Heads = 2/8

  • When we add these options 1/8+ 4/8 + 2/8 = 7/8

  • To find the 3 Heads, we can calculate by 1-7/8 = 1/8.

We can visualize the exact details in graphs. Please refer to Figure 12.1.

Figure 12.1 Example – Random variable

12.3 Expected Value

Once we have a probability distribution for a discrete random variable, X, we can calculate the expected value E(X), the mean of X. We often refer to the expected value as the “long-term average.” When we run an experiment repeatedly, this is the mean we expect to find. To find this value for a discrete random variable, we must “weigh” each value.

For example, we want to find the expected value for the number of heads when we flip a coin three times. In that case, we will multiply each value of X by the corresponding value of P(X) and then add them all together.

X

Probability – Random Variable for Getting Heads When Flip the Coin: P(X)

0

0.125

1

0.5

2

0.25

3

0.125

Table 12.1 Example – Random variable – P(Heads)

Therefore, the expected value is

E(X) = μX = 0(0.125) + 1(0.50) + 2(0.25) + 3(0.125)
μX = 0 + 0.50 + 0.50 + 0.375
μX = 1.375

Therefore, on average, we will expect to get 1.375 heads when we flip a coin three times. We can then extrapolate this to guess that, for example, we should get 1375 heads when we flip a coin 10000 times.

12.4 Variance and Standard Deviation

We can also find the variance and standard deviation for discrete random variables. To find the variance, we will take the difference between X and the mean, μX, square that difference, then multiply the result by the probability of X, called P(X).

We will do that for each value of X and then add all those results to get the variance, σ2X.

Let us find the variance when X is the number of heads we get when we flip a coin three times, remembering that we already found E(X) = 1.375 for this probability distribution.

σ2X = (0 – 1.375)2 (0.125) + (1 – 1.375) 2(0.50) + (2 – 1.375) 2(0.25) + (3 – 1.375) 2(0.125)

Table 12.2 Example – variance

σ2X = 0.734

We can also find the standard deviation of X, σX, which is just the square root of the variance.

σX =

σX ≈ 0.856

12.5 Transforming Random Variables

We discussed shifting and scaling the entire dataset values during the central tendency and spread topic.

Shifting the data set by a constant c means adding c to every value in the data set or subtracting c from every value in the data set. On the other hand, scaling the data set by a constant c means multiplying or dividing every value in the data set by c.

As discussed already, mean, median, and mode usually change according to the change in the dataset. On the other hand, range and IQR will stay the same even though we scale or shift the entire dataset.

For example, in the original dataset, if we have values like below

  • Mean = 10

  • Median = 12

  • Mode = 14

  • IQR = 8

  • Range = 20

Then the shifted values will be like below

  • Mean = 10 + c

  • Median = 12 + c

  • Mode = 14 + c

  • IQR = 8

  • Range = 20

Along with the above data, the standard deviation also remains the same.

The standard deviation (σ) will look like this below (Figure 12.2). The location of the data distribution changes from one location to another when we add constant c. However, the standard deviation never gets changed within the data distributions.

Figure 12.2 Shifting the data set.

However, when we shift the dataset by multiplication and division, IQR, Range also changes. As a result, the standard deviation also gets changed.

For example, in the original dataset, if we have values like below

  • Mean = 10

  • Median = 12

  • Mode = 14

  • IQR = 8

  • Range = 20

  • Standard Deviation = σ

Then the scaled values will be like below (Ex: Multiplication)

  • Mean = 10c

  • Median = 12c

  • Mode = 14c

  • IQR = 8c

  • Range = 20c

  • Standard Deviation = σc

Because the scaling activity will shift the variance and deviation accordingly, Figure 12.3 shows that spread changes when we scale the dataset.

Figure 12.3 Scaling the data set

12.6 Linear Combinations of Random Variables

Whatever we have seen in the previous section was related to scaling or shifting the dataset with constant C. Entire data will change when it scales or shifts. Now we will see how two or more datasets get combined. It is the combination of two or more datasets.

For example, we can take one famous restaurant in Bangalore, India. After collecting all the 365-day data [A year] for the number of breakfast packets sold, we got a dataset. We can get the mean of the dataset and the standard deviation of the dataset; it will look like this below.

μB = 250 and σB = 0.2

At the same time, we also collected the dataset for the quality of the lunch packets sold. We measured the mean and standard deviation of this dataset too.

μL = 150 and σL = 0.1

However, the restaurant owner wants to understand the combined mean and standard deviation. Therefore, he calculates the average of the total packets sold for breakfast and lunch with the help of the below formulas.

We can denote the mean of these combined calculations like below.

  • μA = μB + μL

  • μA = 250 + 150

  • μA = 400

However, Standard deviation cannot be added just like the mean. We need to use the formula below.

  • σA2 = σB2 + σL2

  • So, σA2 = (0.2)2 + (0.1)2

  • σA2 = 0.04 + 0.01

  • σA2 = 0.05

Then to find the standard deviation for total activity, we take the square root of both sides. σA2 = 0.05

σA ≈ 0.22

In addition, we can also calculate the subtraction (The subtraction between two data sets)

We can denote the mean of these different calculations like below.

  • μD= μB – μL

  • μD = 250 – 150

  • μD = 100

Standard deviation is the same as addition. No further changes.

When we combine normally distributed variables, the distribution will also be normal for the combinations.

So, we get the mean and standard deviation of two normally distributed variables. In that case, we can calculate the mean and standard deviation of the new combination.

However, since the distribution is normal for the combination, we can use what we know about the probability under normal distributions to answer probability questions about the combination.

Table 12.3 Linear combinations of the data set

12.7 Permutations and Combinations

We need permutations and combinations of mathematics for many probability and statistical calculations. Many of us would have studied during our school days. So, we will revisit them here in this section.

A permutation means unique order-based combinations. How many numbers of ways can we arrange the set of data elements by keeping particular order in mind?

The formula for a permutation is

n P k = n! / (n − k)!

Here n is the total items in the data set, and k is the number of specific data items we need to arrange.

Example:

There are four shops in the street. Raja has time to visit only three shops in the street. How many different ways can he get it for his visit plan? (Possible order)

He may have ordered the visit plan something like this 123, 124, 234, 231 (If we denote the shop numbers as 1, 2, 3, 4, these are permutations)

So as per permutations calculations,

4 P 3 = 4! / (4 − 3)!
4 P 3 = 4! / 1!
4 P 3 = 4 ⋅ 3 ⋅ 2 ⋅ 1 / 1
4 P 3 = 4 ⋅ 3 ⋅ 2
4 P 3 = 24

So, there are 24 unique orders

Another measurement is a combination. Particular order does not matter here. Just combinations of the available data elements will get calculated.

n C k = n! / k! (n − k)!

Here n is the total items in the data set, and k is the number of specific data items we need to arrange.

Example:

Consider our old example with combinations in mind. There are four shops in the street. Raja has time to visit only three shops in the street. How many different combinations of shops can he visit there?

So as per combinations calculations,

n C k = n! / k! (n − k)!
4 C 3 = 4! / 3! (4 − 3)!
4 C 3 = 4! / (3!) (1!)
4C3 = 4 ⋅ 3 ⋅ 2 ⋅ 1 / (3 ⋅ 2 ⋅ 1) (1)
4C3 = 4 / 1
4C3 = 4

Just 4. Yes. The combinations are (123, 124, 234, 134). Whatever the order we make, it does not matter here in combinations. Just how many combinations we can make matters.

12.8 Binomial Random Variables

Bi means two. So Binomial random variable means it can take only two exact values. It cannot take more than two. The coin will give either heads or tails. Only two values are possible in a coin. So Binomial random variable will work for this.

The die example has six values, and the card example has 52 cards. These cannot fit with binomial random variables. Here binomial random variable will work for any event for which we have only outcomes like “Success” or “Failure” with 50% probability.

12.9 Binomial Random Variable Characters

For a variable X to be a binomial random variable,

  • X should participate in multiple trials of the same action.

  • Each trial must be independent.

  • Each trial can be called a “success” or “failure,”

  • There are a fixed number of trials, and

  • The probability of success on each trial is constant.

We will take one example; Let X be the random variable, and it gets counted as how many heads we can get when we flip the coin 100 times.

  • Here each trial is independent because we get the same coin each time without any change from the previous trial. The coin is a coin that never gets modified when we make trials.

  • Each time we get tails, we say it is a failure. If we get heads, we say that it is a successful one.

  • Fixed number of trails we are trying. Here, 100 times we are tossing the coin.

  • The probability of getting heads will be 50%, the tails also the same, and the probability of getting tails also will be 50%.

All five characters are satisfied here. So, the above example can fit under binomial random variable distribution.

Conversely, we can take another example, different from a binomial random variable.

Let us think about random variable K as how many kings we get when we pull three cards from a deck without replacing the card after each pull.

Here each trial is not independent because we are not replacing the card; whenever we remove one card, the next event will depend on the previous event.

Each time when we get king, it is called success. Otherwise, it is a failed case. This point is satisfying.

We calculate with the help of the fixed number of trails. It is 3. This point also is satisfying.

The probability is not constant. For example, 4/52 is the initial probability, which is not constant.

Condition 1 and condition 4 are not satisfied. So, we cannot use binomial random variable calculations for this random variable.

12.10 Binomial Probability

How do we calculate the probability in binomial? We have a specific formula to calculate the probability of success in fixed attempts. The formula is specified below.

Here, Combination n C k will get calculated for the binomial coefficient.

Example:

Three pens are in a bag: 2 are Ball-Point pens, and 1 is Ink-pen. We will do five trials where we pull a pen, note the type, and then replace the pen. What is the probability that we get the Ink-pen precisely three times?

First, we need to confirm the characters of binomial random variables for the above example.

  • It is independent because we are replacing the pen. This condition is satisfied.

  • Ink pen means success. Ball-point pen means failure. So, we have only two options (Success/failure). This condition is satisfied.

  • Trails are 5. Fixed one. This condition is satisfied.

  • The probability is constant because we are replaying the pen. This condition is also satisfied.

So, it is a binomial distribution only. All four points are satisfied. So, we will use the probability calculation using the formula mentioned above.

The binomial coefficient will be

5C3 = 5! / 3! (5 − 3)!
5C3 = 5! / 3! 2!
5C3 = 10

Using formula

P (3 Ink-pens in 5 Pulls) = (10) * (1/3)3 * (2/3)2 = 440/243 = 16.5%

We can also create a probability distribution for binomial random variables. For example, if we keep the random variable as only Ink-pens, we will have the following options in 5 trials.

  • P (0 Ink-pens in 5 pulls) = 0.1350

  • P (1 Ink-pens in 5 pulls) = 0.3325

  • P (2 Ink-pens in 5 pulls) = 0.3275

  • P (3 Ink-pens in 5 pulls) = 0.1613

  • P (4 Ink-pens in 5 pulls) = 0.0397

  • P (5 Ink-pens in 5 pulls) = 0.0039

12.11 Poisson Distributions

Poisson distribution is another set of models for the discrete random variable. This one is beneficial for finding the probability that a specific number of events will occur in a given period.

We need to understand the poison process before we move into poison probabilities.

The Poisson processes

A Poisson process calculates the number of times an event occurs for a specific period. It could be in a particular area, or some particular distance, or within any other measurement. This process also has some characteristics. We will discuss these characters below.

  • If we keep multiple intervals, the mean should be equal for all the intervals.

  • The experiment counts the number of occurrences of an event over some other measurement.

  • Interval-wise, independence will be there. There is no dependency between the intervals.

  • The intervals should not overlap.

  • The probability of the event occurring is proportional to the period.

Example of poison process and distributions

  • The number of visitors to a movie theatre for the month. We need to collect the statistics for the day-wise count.

  • The number of trees in an acre of forest.

We will take one exponential poison example and check whether the above characters satisfy this example. We will calculate the number of visitors to a movie theatre example.

  • We are counting the visitors for every show. The interval is the day. So, the mean number of visitors of the day is 300. Moreover, the same mean is getting maintained for the whole month. So, this is satisfactory.

  • When we say interval as day, we can calculate the event. The event is a show. So, a second condition is also satisfied.

  • Intervals are independent because shows are unique. So, a third condition is also satisfied.

  • Movie shows do not overlap. So, the fourth condition is also satisfied.

The probability of the event occurring is propositional to the period where it matches. For example, if 100 people attend the theatre (C) for one show, it will be 200 (2C) for two. So, the probability is proportional to the specific period.

Since we met all the conditions, we can use the poison process and poison probability for further calculations.

Probability formula for Poisson:

Suppose we have precisely x occurrences in the particular event. In that case, the probability of that event will get calculated from the below formula. Here, the mean number of occurrences in the interval is λ, and we need this data for calculation.

P(x) = λx e−λ / x!

So, if we believe the mean number of visitors to that movie theatre for the day is 15, we can denote this as λ = 15. If we want to know the probability that x = 13 visitors will come the next day, then this probability will be

P (13) = 1513e-15 / 13!
P (13) = 9.56%

So, there is an approximately 9.56 % chance that 13 visitors will arrive the next day.

We generally use the Poisson formula to calculate the cumulative probabilities. For instance, if we want to know the probability that at most seven visitors arrive for the day at a movie theatre, we calculate that as

P (x ≤ 7) = P (x = 0) + P (x = 1) + P (x = 2) + P (x = 3) 182 +P (x = 4) + P (x = 5) + P (x = 6) + P (x = 7)

P (x ≤ 7) = 150e-15 / 0! + 151e-15 / 1! + 152e-15 / 2! + 153e-15 / 3! + 154e-15 / 4!

+ 155e-15 / 5! + 156e-15 / 6! + 157e-15 / 7!

P (x ≤ 7) ≈ 0.018

So, there is an approximately 1.8 % chance that seven or fewer visitors will arrive at the movie theatre.

Poisson distribution to approximate the binomial distribution:

We already know how to calculate binomial probabilities, but using the Poisson distribution instead can save us some time.

The Poisson distribution very closely approximates the binomial distribution when the number of binomial trials n is at least 20, and the probability of success p in the binomial trial is at most 0.05.

If those two conditions meet here, then using the Poisson probability formula will give us an excellent approximation of the binomial probability. Moreover, the math for the Poisson probability is more accessible than the binomial probability, so that we will save some time. The probability of x successes in n attempts, given by the Poisson formula, is

P(x) = ((np) x e−np) / x!

Notice that we have substituted λ = np into the original Poisson formula.

“At least” and “at most.”

Most of the time, we say “at least” and “at most” in probability. Let us say that at least this random variable gets five as a sum. We calculate the probability behind all the options in that random variable. If we say, at most, this random variable gets five as the sum, we calculate the probability for all the below options in that random variable.

We have seen this example in the previous poison example.

For instance, if we want to know the probability that at most seven visitors arrive for the day at a movie theatre, we calculate that as

P (x ≤ 7) = P (x = 0) + P (x = 1) + P (x = 2) + P (x = 3) 182 +P (x = 4) + P (x = 5) + P (x = 6) + P (x = 7)

P (x ≤ 7) = 150e-15 / 0! + 151e-15 / 1! + 152e-15 / 2! + 153e-15 / 3! + 154e-15 / 4!

+ 155e-15 / 5! + 156e-15 / 6! + 157e-15 / 7!

P (x ≤ 7) ≈ 0.018

Mean, variance, and standard deviation

The mean of a binomial random variable X expresses as μX.

The mean is also called the expected value, which denotes E(X). The means are specified below

μX = E(X) = np

where n is the fixed number of independent trials, and p is the probability of success. The variance of a binomial random variable X is

σ2X = np (1 – p)

Standard deviation is the square root of the variance and is therefore given by

12.12 Bernoulli Random Variables

Bernoulli’s random variable is the same as the binomial random variable except for several trials. We have this significant difference here. Bernoulli random variable means it deals with only one trial. We hope the reader remembers that binomial random variable deals with multiple trials. Rest all conditions are the same in Bernoulli. Only two options must be there as options (Success or Failure)

Bernoulli Distributions

Imagine, when we go to any cricket stadium, we can see that all the fans will support either one of the teams. Considering that no one is neutral, the complete probability is 100% (the probability for team A and the probability of team B) within the stadium.

If we take the survey, 25% of fans support Team A, and 75% support Team B. This distribution is called the Bernoulli distribution. The visualization of the Bernoulli distribution is specified below in Figure 12.4.

Figure 12.4 Example – Bernoulli Distributions

Mean, variance, and standard deviation for a mean of Bernoulli Distributions

We can take the probability-weighted sum of the values in our Bernoulli distribution for the mean. If we take the above cricket stadium example,

μ = (percentage of failures) (0) + (percentage of successes) (1)

μ = (0.25) (0) + (0.75) (1)

μ = 0 + 0.75

μ = 0.75

It is the mean of the Bernoulli distribution. Notice how the value we found for the mean equals the percentage of “successes.”

In general, it is success probability.

μ = p

For variance, also the similar way we get the output

σ2 = (0.25) (0 − μ)2 + (0.75) (1 − μ)2

σ2 = 0.1875

After taking root of the above variance, we get the standard deviation. The standard deviation value will be

σ = 0.4330

In general, as with the mean in Bernoulli Distributions, the variance can be calculated only from the success probability of that event, as below.

σ2 = p (1 – p)

13

Statistical Methods: Sampling





13.1 The Art of Collecting Statistical Data

Whatever we have studied so far is very important in statistics. However, all those logics will be perfect only if the data set is appropriately collected. The study of data must be accurate. We are collecting excellent and reliable data from the samples. In most cases, the population data is vast in numbers. Therefore, we usually collect samples, make decisions from sample statistics and derive the answers for population data statistics.

13.2 The Goal of Collecting Samples

The purpose of statistics is to gather information (data) about the world around us and analyse it to help make sense of it.

As we mentioned in the previous paragraphs, collecting the population is impossible. Therefore, we chose a smaller sample of the large population and then analyse the data for the collected samples. Finally, we hope that our results will translate to the larger population.

Usually, mean and standard deviation are very important. These characteristics are called sample statistics from a sample perspective. A parameter is the corresponding characteristic of the population that the statistic is trying to estimate. So, we could choose a sample, calculate the sample mean (a statistic), and then use what we know about the sample mean to make inferences about the population mean (a parameter)

It means that we collect the sample data, calculate the sample mean and infer the population means with the help of some effective techniques.

So, before all, we need to know how we collect the samples.

13.3 Observational Study and Experimental Study

An observational study is a method that will look into the information already there. We are not modifying anything in the population or sample data set. Instead, just like the third-party statistics collector, we collect the data. This method is called an observational study.

For example, go to some city streets and collect statistics from people about their interest in mangos. Since this street is the sample, whatever we collect will replicate the entire city population (population data). However, we are not modifying anything and just observing the data.

Opposite to an observational study is an experimental study. In an experiment, we manipulate what is happening and try to establish causality, not just correlation.

For example, people get sample statistics for innovative medicine in the medical field. Then, we inject innovative medicine into the sample people. It is called an experimental study.

13.4 One-Way Tables and Two-Way Tables

As mentioned above, most of the time, single data will get collected, like collecting the mango’s interest in the sample street. These are called one-way tables.

Instead of this, if we collect two data from samples and connect or correlate both data in statistical studies and analysis, these are called two-way tables.

In the one-way table also, the unbiased collection is mandatory. The representations must be random. For example, if we had collected the statistics from only above 50 ages, this would be biased because 50% of these 50-age people will have diabetic complaints. Diabetic people will not consume mangos. Then this needs to be more objective, and the statistics employees must collect from random people.

Also, we do not observe anything else in the one-way table, other than single inference.

Instead of this one-way table approach, if we additionally collect more data from people, like whether they are diabetic compliant, it will be good. We may correlate the mango’s interest and diabetic complaint. We need to observe this correlation from the sample studies.

Two variables are correlated when they move together predictably. The variables are positively correlated when they increase together or decrease together. Variables are negatively correlated when they increase and decrease in opposite directions: one goes down while the other goes up or up while the other goes down.

On the other hand, causation means that one variable causes another variable to change. However, just because we show correlation does not mean we have proven causation. If we want more details about this paragraph, do not worry. We are going to discuss this in the next chapter.

13.5 Exclusive to Experimental Studies

For any experiment, we need to assign people into two groups. So, we use random sampling techniques. It means we will choose people from all categories (regardless of age, sex, or religion). So that groups are not biased.

One group is a control group, and we are not doing anything with them. It means that we will not conduct any experiments with them. Another group is the actual experimental group. With this group only, we do the experiments. Why do we need a control group? It is required to make people blind. Nobody knows whether we have given the sample group vitamin tablets or natural medicine. So that no one will influence the results, the little influence will be there with patients, doctors, and all parties in the experiment. For example, suppose the patient says he got a negative effect due to the experiment. In that case, the doctor will also favour him from the mind. Here, we need some confidential data from everyone during the experiment. That is why we have a control group.

13.6 Sampling and Bias

Picking random data is very important. If we take our previous example, we can understand that we need to collect statistics from all diversified people. We will always have a bias when we collect the data. If we precisely match the population perfectly, that is called unbiased sample collection. Bias also has many reasons. Random sampling agents must avoid this.

13.7 Sampling Techniques

Simple random sample: If we directly divide the groups from samples, this is called a simple random sample. For example, when we enter the street, we randomly send two groups of samplers to houses. However, finally, at the end of the sampling activity, we found that group 1 collected the details from 95% of men. In this case, this random fashion is not correct. They got this bad split from the random houses. It is a big issue with simple random techniques.

Stratified random sample: Instead of direct random, we can put some parameters in control for Stratified random samples. Say when we send groups for sampling, ask them to collect the data in the Stratified controls (Sample Stratified: 50% men and 50% women; 20% child and 30% youth; 30% mid-age and 20% old age; Rich houses: 50% and poor houses:50%). Practically they collect the samples from this category with the specified ratio.

Clustered random sample: Imagine we need to collect samples from school students about traffic jams. If we randomly collect the samples from village schools, it does not matter. So even for population data, there are some clusters. If the population identifies clusters naturally, we perform the clustered random sample method. Here Stratified random methodology can be used.

13.8 Sampling Distributions of the Sample Mean

As we discussed multiple times, collecting the entire population data is difficult. We usually collect the samples and measure mean, variance, and standard deviation from this sample data. However, these results are different from population results.

For example, if we collect the height of the eleventh-grade students from one school, the mean value will be 5.1 feet. However, it will not be with the actual population mean if the population is the entire country. Then the sample data will not get matched.

‘N’ is the population count, and ‘n’ is the sample count. So even though we collect the perfect representative from random sampling, the mean value will need to be more accurate.

We can discuss this sample-population issue with one minor dataset.

In one particular class, we have 40 students with a mean height of 65 inches. Consider these 40 students as a population. However, we plan to collect the sample survey only from 4 students. Initially, we get the data from the tallest girls. The sample’s mean would not be a reasonable estimate of the population mean because the mean height of the sample is 217 inches which is very far from the population means. Similarly, if we instead happened to choose the four shortest girls, the sample mean would be much lower than the actual population means.

So how can we correct this issue?

Instead of only one sample, we can take multiple samples from the population. For example, instead of picking only four students as one sample here, we can pick multiple sample groups. The total number of possible samples is (Nn), where N is the total population from which we take our samples, and n is the sample size.

In our example, Sample ‘n’ is 4, and the population ‘N’ is 40. If we calculate this, we get a total of (404) = 2,560,000 possible samples that can be taken from the 40-member class. Consider that this example is talking about sampling with replacement. It means we are replacing the samples with the population and collecting the following sample. The same sample elements may reappear in the new sample combination.

The fascinating thing is this. Imagine if we are collecting all the possible 2,560,000 samples and get the sample means. These means fit in a normal distribution. It means that this normal distribution is not related to our actual values. This normal distribution is for the mean value of samples. It is called the Sampling distribution of sample mean (SDSM).

Figure 13.1 Sampling distribution of sample mean (SDSM)

Refer to Figure 13.1 for the sample mean (SDSM) sampling distribution. Just think about the sampling distribution of the sample mean as the probability distribution of all possible values of the sample mean .

We do not need to take all possible sample combinations for calculations. Instead, we can take enough samples to calculate the statistics. For example, suppose we assume the population is the normal distribution and use a sample size more significant than 30 (n ≥ 30); we can take the sampling distribution. Here, the sampling distribution of the sample mean is average. From this sampling distribution statical calculations, we can answer any probability questions.

Central limit theorem

The sampling distribution of the sample mean is normal only if the original population fits under the normal distribution. Here sample size does not matter. Even though the sample size is small, the sample mean (SDSM) sampling distribution will fit under the normal distribution. It is happening because the original population itself fits within normal distribution.

Most of the time, the original distribution will not fit under the normal distribution. In that case, the Sampling distribution of the sample mean (SDSM) will fit under normal distribution for more extensive size samples (n ≥ 30). Otherwise, if the sample size is below 30, SDSM will not fit under normal for the non-normal population.

Most populations (about 90%) do not fit under normal distributions. Normal distribution means exact bell-shaped distribution. It is only possible in some cases. Most of the population fits under non-normal distributions.

The central limit theorem tells us to convert the non-normal population distribution into normal sampling distribution of the sample means using the abovementioned logic. That is, taking a sample count of more than 30 will help us to get the normal distribution for SDSM (n ≥ 30). If we take a sample count of more than 30 for multiple samples, automatically, these sample mean fits under normal distributions.

So, the Central Limit Theorem is helpful because it lets us apply what we know about normal distributions, like the properties of mean, variance, and standard deviation, to non-normal populations.

Mean, variance, and standard deviation

The central limit theorem also tells us that the mean of all the sampling distributions of the sample mean will always be the same as the mean of the corresponding population. It means that

Coming to variance analysis, we can calculate the SDSM variance by using the following formula only if we know the population variance. It will work if we replace the sample with the population for all sampling. Also, it will work when the population is infinite.

However, most of the time, we will not get the population variance. No one will give this information. In these cases, we will measure the sampling variance. We need to consider this sampling variance as population variance.

We call the standard deviation of the sampling distribution for the sample mean (SDSM) as sampling error. Refer to Figure 13.1. It means the standard deviation of sample means. We also call this a standard error of the mean. The formula for calculating that is as

The above formula will work only when we use population standard deviation σ. Here n is the sample size. We can still use the sample standard deviation if we do not know the population standard deviation. It will help us to match the population standard deviation.

The standard error helps us to match the sample mean with the population mean. Here we mention the same error because we say how this sample deviated from the population. For example, 40 students with standard heights will be in the class. If we take the sample of 4 incredibly tall students, then this sample has completely deviated from the average crowd. So, this sample means deviated from the population crowd. So, we call it a significant error from the population average.

When the standard error is more extensive, the sample means in the SDSM are more spread out, making it less likely that any given sample mean accurately represents the actual population mean. However, when the standard error is minor, the sample means are less spread out, making it more likely that any given sample mean accurately represents the actual population mean.

Usually, the standard error will get an immense value when the population standard deviation is significant. Also, the standard error will get a considerable value when the sample size is small. Say less than 30.

Conversely, the standard error will get a small value when the population standard deviation is slight. Also, the standard error will get a small value when the sample size is big. Say more than 30.

Finite population correction factor:

The value of the standard deviation of the sampling distribution of the sample mean (Standard Error) is

The above formula needs to be more accurate. We need to correct something to make it perfect.

The finite population correction factor must be added here in the standard error formula. However, this factor needs to be added only in a few conditions. The conditions are specified below.

When the population is finite, we use a sample size of more than 5% of the finite population.

We only replace the samples after taking the subsequent samples.

In all other cases, we do not need this correction factor, and we can directly use the formula without this correction factor.

Only some examples are infinite population, sampling replacement cases, and small samples from population count.

As with standard deviation, variance also gets a similar correction from the Finite population correction factor.

Conditions for Sampling distributions of the sample mean (SDSM) inference:

The central limit theorem tells us we can use SDSM for population analysis. We need population data analysis. These samples and sampling distribution help us to make inferences very clearly.

For example, we want to know the population mean. In that case, we can take the sample mean and fit the sample mean in the sample distribution of the sample mean (SDSM). When comparing our sample mean against to mean of the sample distribution of sample mean (SDSM). This comparison will help us in population mean analysis.

However, we must satisfy the conditions below for accurate analysis and inference. These are valid, and they will make valid results against to population.

  • The sample count should be more significant. It should have more than 30 counts.

  • The sample size should be more than 10% of the population count.

  • The sample should be random.

The sample count should be more significant. It should have more than 30 counts:

The sample count should be more than 30 for the non-normal distribution of the population. However, if it is under normal distribution, we do not need to be strict with n>=30.

We also have a finite population correction factor in case the sample is more significant than 5% of the finite population.

The sample size should be at most 10% of the population count:

This condition is mainly applicable when we have sampled with replacement. The samples should be independent. That is the main reason we are keeping this rule. It is applicable when we have a finite population.

Z-Score analysis for SDSM

We already know that our SDSM is a normal distribution at any cost. We are confirming this from the central limit theorem. Therefore, we have normal or non-normal distribution; we will have a normal distribution for SDSM. The only requirement is sample size should be more significant than 30 for non-normal population distributions.

Suppose we meet all the SDSM conditions we have discussed before this section. In that case, we can use the sampling distribution of the sample mean to answer questions about the probability that any given sample mean will fall within some distance of the population mean. 13.9 Sampling Distribution of the Sample Proportion (SDSP)

As with the mean, proportion also gets calculated in the same way. It means that we can find the sampling distribution of the sampling proportion (SDSP) in the same way that we have calculated the sampling distribution of the sampling mean (SDSM).

Here we are working on population proportion p instead of the population mean µ. To get this answer as the sampling distribution of the sampling mean (SDSM), we are working on the sampling distribution of the sampling proportion (SDSP).

Sampling distribution of the sampling proportion (SDSP)

Usually, proportion means how many subjects in the survey meet some specific condition. For example, In the street, the statistical engineer found that 50 people liked mangos from 100 people crowd, and he took this data from the sampling survey. Sample proportion usually uses the below formula.

We create the sampling distribution of the sample proportion (SDSP) when we take every possible sample from our population, calculate the sample proportion for each sample, and plot all of those proportions into a probability distribution.

Therefore, the SDSP is the probability distribution of all possible sample proportions .

Central limit theorem

In proportion, everything is binary—something like “whether this proportion succeeded or failed”. So here, we are not going to use the normal distribution. Instead, since this is discrete, we will use binomial distribution.

Even though the population proportion uses binomial distribution, our central limit theorem still works for samples. It means the samples will maintain the normal distribution in the sampling distribution of the sampling proportion (SDSP). One condition is that the sample size should be more than 30.

Conditions for sampling distributions of the sample proportions (SDSP) inference

As with Sampling distributions of the sample mean (SDSM), the SDSP also gets satisfied with a few conditions to get an accurate analysis of population proportions.

  • The sample count should be more significant. The “np” count should be more than 5.

  • The sample size should be at most 10% of the population count.

  • The sample should be random.

The sampling distribution of the sample proportion will have perfection if np >=5 and n(1-p) >=5. So here, ‘n’ is the sample size, and ‘p’ is the population proportion.

If np ≥ 5 is true, it tells us that we can expect to have at least 5 “successes” in the sample, and if n (1 − p) ≥ 5 is true, it tells us that we can expect to have at least 5 “failures.

So, if our sample size is n = 1000 and the population proportion is p = 20 %, then we multiply 1000 by 0.2 and 1 − 0.2 = 0.8 to ensure both values are at least 5.

1000 ⋅ 0.8 = 800 > 5 and 10 ⋅ 0.2 = 200 > 5

The sample size should not cross above 10% of the population
count.

This condition is mainly applicable when we have sampled with replacement. The samples should be independent. That is the main reason we are keeping this rule. It is applicable when we have a finite population.

13.10 The Student’s t-Distribution

Figure 13.2 The student’s t-distribution

We have already seen that standard normal distribution is helping us a lot in many aspects. The standard normal distribution is also getting called z-distribution. It is symmetrical bell-shaped distribution, and we have mean µ and standard deviation σ.

We also saw that we need to use z-tables to get the percentile values using z-score values. The normal distribution also has a little standard deviation in the bell curve. By the Empirical rule, about 68 % of the area under the standard normal distribution is found between the z-scores z = − 1 and z = 1, about 95 % of the area resides between z = − 2 and z = 2, and about 99.7 % of the area resides is between z = − 3 and z = 3. Here Z-scores mean standard deviation.

As with z-distribution, we also have one more similar distribution named t-distribution. T-distribution is an excellent alternative to z-distribution. From many interesting cases, t-distribution will help us a lot. This section will look at all the features and benefits individually.

The student’s t-distribution is similar to the standard normal distribution (i.e., z – distribution) in that it is symmetrical, bell-shaped, and centered around the mean μ = 0.

However, the significant difference in t-distribution is the edges. However, it is flatter and wider. Wider means it never lies down. Slowly it got down due to the main reason. The standard deviation is profound. Both these properties help us to handle any data. Even with the small sample sizes, we can work with t-distribution (Even if n>=30).

More areas in T-distribution get speared in the tail part. Please refer to Figure 13.2 for the distribution split and the difference. Also, most of the time, we call this distribution a T-distribution.

Another exciting part of t-distribution is that we do not need to have only one generic distribution like Z-Distribution. We use the degrees of freedom concept here. It is n-1, where n is our sample size. This degree of freedom is getting used to changing the T-distribution’s broader pattern. If DOF is 1, the T-distribution’s more comprehensive range will be too deep. When the DOF is 30, the wider T-distribution will equal the Z-distribution. Slowly the broader curve will come to normal distribution based on the DOF (From 1-30). For all above 30 DOF, the T-distribution will get used as Z – distribution. Please refer to Figure 13.3 to understand T-distribution in deep. So, the t-distribution for a sample size n = 15 (with n − 1 = 15 − 1 = 14 degrees of freedom) will look different than the t-distribution for a sample of size n = 25 (with n − 1 = 25 − 1 = 24 degrees of freedom)

Figure 13.3 The growing t-distribution

We must mainly use T-distribution when our sample size is less than 30. if we use a sample size n < 30, we should calculate a t-score and look up that t-score in a t-table instead of calculating a z-score and looking up that z-score in a z-table.

If n ≥ 30 → Find a z-score, look it up in the z-table

If n < 30 → Find a t-score and look it up in the t-table. As mentioned above, both T-distribution and Z-distribution will become equal after we use a sample size of more than 30.

Finding values in the t-table:

Looking up t-values in a t-table is similar to looking up z-values in a z-table. To find values in the t-table, we need to know the degrees of freedom (n − 1, when the sample size is n) and either the upper-tail probability or the confidence level.

Figure 13.4 The t-distribution score table

13.11 Confidence Interval for the Mean

How confident we are with the sample mean and sample proportion values. Even though we use the central limit theorem and perfect probability-based lookup, we get a single-point outcome. It is because we usually follow all the pre-conditions and calculate using Z-tables and T-tables to gain confidence. However, as we mentioned before, a sample mean or sample proportion might be an excellent estimate of the population parameter or a terrible estimate. So, it would be beneficial to say how confident we are about how well the sample statistic estimates the population parameter. That is where confidence intervals come in.

Instead of the single point, we can calculate the confidence intervals for the population analysis; It means we can calculate the population means and population proportion with some confident interval range.

Usually, the point estimate for the population mean, μ, is the sample mean , and the point estimate for the population standard deviation, σ, is the sample standard deviation, s

Point estimation is easy to get calculated. Interval estimation is more complex than point estimation. However, this will give confidence. A point estimate does not tell us how good or bad the estimate is.

Interval confidence → Instead of saying that this is the population mean and proportion, we can confidently say the interval data, like below.

  • “We are 98% confident with the population mean, which fits in the interval value (X, Y)”.

  • “We are 99% confident with the population proportion, which fits the interval value (X, Y)”.

The confidence interval is the probability of the population parameters. It is most common to choose 99 %, 98 %, or 99.5 % as the confidence level and then find the interval associated with that confidence level.

Alpha α and the region of rejection: For example, we will get a 95% confidence level for our population calculation. It means that 5 % of the confidence intervals we find will not contain the population parameter. This 5 % (or 10 % for a 90 % confidence level, or 1 % for a 99 % confidence level) is called the alpha value, α. We also call α the significance level or the probability of making a Type I error (more on Type I and Type II errors later).

Therefore, α is always mentioned or calculated as a “1 − confidence level”. It means that the 1 – α confidence interval has a significant level of α.

We can visualize α as the total area under the normal distribution outside the confidence interval. For instance, given a 95 % confidence level, the alpha value is α = 1 − 0.95 α = 0.05.

Since the confidence interval is always surrounding near the mean of the normal distribution, we can show the centre 95 % of the distribution, with half of α in the lower tail to the left of the confidence interval and the other half of α in the upper tail to the right of the confidence interval. See below Figure 13.5 for this distribution.

Figure 13.5 Example – Confidence interval for the mean

At a 90 % confidence level, we can expect the smallest 2.5 % and largest 2.5 % values to fall outside the confidence interval because α is split evenly into the upper and lower tails, and α/2 = 0.05/2 = 0.025.

Using a z-table, the z-values associated with −0.025 and +0.025 are −1.96 and +1.96, respectively. It means the boundaries of the 95 % confidence interval are −zα/2 = − 1.95 and zα/2 = + 1.95.

After getting the above values, we can calculate the region of rejection. ±zα/2 are the boundaries of the region of rejection. For 95% confidence levels, the outside of ± 1.95 Z-values are getting the confidence.

14

Statistical Methods: Hypothesis Testing





14.1 Inferential Statistics and Hypotheses

Whatever we have seen in statistics, all are related to solutions. However, before the solutions, we need to define the requirements. Isn’t it?

We are discussing it in reverse. We have discussed the solutions so far. However, the query matters a lot. We need to toss one statement about the population and verify whether this statement is true. That is the process. To prove that statement, we need to pick some samples from the population, prepare the statistics and conclude whether the statement is valid for our population.

For example, we are tossing this statement “40% middle age men in India have a beard”. First, it is just a statement. To confirm this statement, we cannot survey the entire population. So, we go for samples. Samples will teach us some probabilities, and finally, we will analyse the statement against sample statistics.

This entire process is called hypothesis testing. From now on, we will see about this hypothesis testing in detail.

There are five steps for hypothesis testing:

  1. State the null and alternative hypotheses.
  2. Determine the level of significance.
  3. Calculate the test statistic.
  4. Find critical values and determine the regions of acceptance and rejection.
  5. State the conclusion

We call this process inferential statistics because we use the information about the sample to make inferences about the population.

If we revisit our example, which we have mentioned above, “40% middle age men in India have beards” is the hypothesis statement. We could take random men for a survey and look at their appearance (whether they have a beard or not). If the percentage in middle age beard category comes to around 39%, then build a confidence interval to state how confident we are with this sample, which produced = 0.39, supports the claim that p = 0.40

Proving the claim is different from providing support. We always need to figure out sample statistics data. We are just providing some data to support the statement. The confidence interval gives little confidence about the data. That is why we call this hypothesis testing.

14.2 Hypothesis

In the hypothesis world of statistics, we have two opposing statements. One is the Null hypothesis, and the other is the Alternative hypothesis. We denote the Null hypothesis as H0 and the alternative hypothesis as H1 (Some people call it Ha)

Here alternate hypothesis means abnormal to general belief. For example, people generally believe that 80% of students study well in that school. Suppose we toss the alternative hypothesis that the school students are not studying well and the good students are 80%. In that case, everyone will shock because we are tossing up new, unusual statements. (When Galileo mentioned that the earth was spherical, everyone was shocked).

The alternative to alternate hypotheses is the null hypothesis. – Which is the current one or which everyone believes!

For example, let us say the good student ratio is below 80% in that school. Then, the null hypothesis will be “80% or Above 80% of students are studying well”.

  • H1: The excellent student ratio is below 80% in that school

  • Ho: 80% or Above 80% of students are studying well

We always test the null hypothesis H0, not the alternative hypothesis H1. If our sample gives us enough evidence, we can reject the null hypothesis and provide evidence supporting our alternative hypothesis.

We will focus on hypothesis tests about two population parameters:

  • The population mean μ

  • The population proportion p

14.3 The Population Mean μ

It is straightforward. If we discuss population, we can also talk about its average value. The average life span of a human being is 82. Suppose we provide the alternate hypotheses statement that the average life span of a human being is below 82. In that case, the null hypothesis will be

“Ho: 82”.

Alternative or null hypotheses can claim the rules with any of the below values.

≤, =, or ≥

The null hypothesis always says that the population mean (or parameter) is normal; nothing new or different is happening.

Here, we must understand that the alternative and null hypotheses are opposite, so if one hypothesis chooses one of the below operators, the corresponding opposite operator needs to be assessed.

“It is not accurate to 40%. It will be below 40% or above 40%.”

This example comes under the “< and >” category of the operator. Just see below Table 14.2 with the highlighted row. Here Null hypotheses must take “=” as the operator. (To make it opposite for < and >)
